The Higman-McLaughlin Theorem for the flag-transitive 2 2 -designs with λ \lambda prime
Alessandro Montinaro

Abstract
. A famous result of Higman and McLaughlin [ 26 ] in 1961 asserts that any flag-transitive automorphism group G G of a 2 2 -design 𝒟 \mathcal{D} with λ = 1 \lambda=1 acts point-primitively on 𝒟 \mathcal{D} . In this paper, we show that the Higman and McLaughlin theorem is still true when λ \lambda is a prime and 𝒟 \mathcal{D} is not isomorphic to one of the two 2 2 - ( 16 , 6 , 2 ) (16,6,2) designs as in [ 42 , Section 1.2] , or the 2 2 - ( 45 , 12 , 3 ) (45,12,3) design as in [ 44 , Construction 4.2] , or, when 2 2 j + 1 2^{2^{j}}+1 is a Fermat prime, a possible 2 2 - ( 2 2 j + 1 ​ ( 2 2 j + 2 ) , 2 2 j ​ ( 2 2 j + 1 ) , 2 2 j + 1 ) (2^{2^{j+1}}(2^{2^{j}}+2),2^{2^{j}}(2^{2^{j}}+1),2^{2^{j}}+1) design having very specific features.



MSC 2020: Mathematics Subject Classification
05B05; 05B25; 20B25

MSC 2020: Mathematics Subject Classification
05B05; 05B25; 20B25

1.
Introduction and Main Result

A
2
-
(v,k,\lambda)

design

\mathcal{D}
is a pair
(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{B})
with a set
\mathcal{P}
of
v
points and a set
\mathcal{B}

of
b
blocks such that each block is a
k
-subset of
\mathcal{P}
and each two distinct points are contained in
\lambda
blocks. The
replication number

r
of
\mathcal{D}
is the number of blocks containing a given point.
We say
\mathcal{D}
is
non-trivial
if
2<k<v
, and
symmetric
if
v=b
. An automorphism of
\mathcal{D}
is a permutation of the point set which preserves the block
set. The set of all automorphisms of
\mathcal{D}
with the composition of
permutations forms a group, denoted by
\mathrm{Aut(\mathcal{D})}
. For a
subgroup
G
of
\mathrm{Aut(\mathcal{D})}
,
G
is said to be
point-primitive
if
G
acts primitively on
\mathcal{P}
, and said to be

point-imprimitive
otherwise. In this setting, we also say that
\mathcal{D}
is either
point-primitive
or
point-imprimitive
, respectively. A
flag
of
\mathcal{D}
is a pair
(x,B)
where
x
is
a point and
B
is a block containing
x
. If
G\leqslant\mathrm{Aut(\mathcal{D})}
acts transitively on the set of flags of
\mathcal{D}
, then we say that
G
is
flag-transitive
and that
\mathcal{D}
is a
flag-transitive design
.

A famous result of Higman and McLaughlin
[
26
]
in 1961 asserts that, if
\lambda=1
, that is
\mathcal{D}
is a linear space, then
G
acts point-primitively on
\mathcal{D}
. Since then, several authors investigated under which conditions on
\mathcal{D}
or on
G
this implication is still true that a flag-transitive automorphism group
G
of
2
-
(v,k,\lambda)
design a
\mathcal{D}
acts point-primitively on
\mathcal{D}
. In his famous book
Finite Geometries
, Dembowski
[
15
]
provided several conditions on the parameters of
\mathcal{D}
such that the flag-transitivity implies the point-primitivity. More recently, Zhong and Zhou
[
12
]
proved that above implication is true when
(v-1,k-1)\leqslant(v-1)^{1/2}
. However, as shown by Davies in
[
14
]
, in general it is not true that point-primitivity arises from flag-transitivity for
\lambda>1
. Nevertheless, Devillers and Praeger
[
17
]
in 2022 showed that the Higman-McLaughling theorem can be extended to the case
\lambda\leqslant 4
except for
v\in\{15,16,36,45,96\}
and
\mathcal{D}
is isomorphic to eleven specific
2
-designs. In the present paper, we move further by showing that the conclusion of the Higman-McLaughling theorem still holds when
\lambda
is a prime number except when
\mathcal{D}
is isomorphic to three specific symmetric
2
-designs,
\lambda
is a Fermat prime greater than 3 and
\mathcal{D}
is a
2
-design with very specific features and whose existence is open. More precisely, we obtain the following result:

Theorem 1.1
.

Let
\mathcal{D}
be a
2
-
(v,k,\lambda)
design with
\lambda
prime admitting a flag-transitive point-imprimitive automorphism group. Then one of the
following holds:

(1)

\mathcal{D}
is one of the two
2
-
(16,6,2)
designs as in
[
42
, Section 1.2]
;

(2)

\mathcal{D}
is the
2
-
(45,12,3)
design as in
[
44
, Construction 4.2]
.

(3)

\mathcal{D}
is a
2
-
(2^{2^{j+1}}(2^{2^{j}}+2),2^{2^{j}}(2^{2^{j}}+1),2^{2^{j}}+1)
design when
2^{2^{j}}+1>3
is a Fermat prime.

More details on
(\mathcal{D},G)
when
\mathcal{D}
is a
2
-design as in (3) of Theorem
1.1
can be found in Theorem
9.2
. The existence of such
2
-designs is investigated by the author in a forthcoming paper. Combining Theorem
1.1
with
[
62
, Theorem]
and
[
63
, Theorem 1]
, we also obtain the following result.

Corollary 1.2
.

Let
\mathcal{D}
be a
2
-
(v,k,\lambda)
design with
\lambda
prime admitting a flag-transitive automorphism group
G
. If
\mathcal{D}
not isomorphic to the two
2
-
(16,6,2)
designs as in
[
42
, Section 1.2]
, or to
2
-
(45,12,3)
design as in
[
44
, Construction 4.2]
, or, when
2^{2^{j}}+1>3
is a Fermat prime, to possible
2
-
(2^{2^{j+1}}(2^{2^{j}}+2),2^{2^{j}}(2^{2^{j}}+1),2^{2^{j}}+1)
design with very specific features, then
G
acts point-primitively on
\mathcal{D}
. Moreover, either
G
is of affine type, or
G
is an almost simple group.

1.1.
Structure of the paper and outline of the proof.

The paper consists of nine sections briefly described below. In Section 1, we introduce the problem, present the state of the art, and state our main results: Theorem
1.1
and Corollary
1.2
. In section 2, we provide some useful reduction results in both design theory and group theory. In particular, we rely on the following three results:

(1)

Theorem of Dembowski
[
15
, 2.3.7(a)]
: any flag-transitive automorphism group
G
of a
2
-
(v,k,\lambda)
design
\mathcal{D}
with
(r,\lambda)=1
acts point-primitively.

(2)

Theorem of Devillers-Praeger
[
17
, Theorem 1]
: if
\mathcal{D}
is any
2
-
(v,k,\lambda)
design admitting a flag-transitive point-imprimitive automorphism group
G
, then
v\leqslant 2\lambda^{2}(\lambda-1)
.

(3)

Theorem of Camina-Zieschang
[
12
, Propositions 2.1 and 2.3]
: let
\mathcal{D}=(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{B})
be any
2-(v,k,\lambda)
design admitting a
flag-transitive, point-imprimitive automorphism group
G
preserving a nontrivial
partition
\Sigma
of
\mathcal{P}
with
v_{1}
classes of size
v_{0}
, where
v=v_{0}v_{1}
. Then there is a constant
k_{0}\geqslant 2
such that
\left|B\cap\Delta\right|=0
or
k_{0}
for each
B\in\mathcal{B}
and
\Delta\in\Sigma
, the parameter
k_{0}
divides
k
. Moreover, the following hold:

(a)

\mathcal{D}_{\Delta}=(\Delta,\mathcal{B}_{\Delta})
with
\Delta\in\Sigma
and
\mathcal{B}_{\Delta}=\left\{B\cap\Delta\neq\varnothing:B\in\mathcal{B}\right\}
, is either a
2
-
(v_{0},k_{0},\lambda_{0})
design or a symmetric
1
-design with
v_{0}
points and
k_{0}=v_{0}-1
. In both cases,
G_{\Delta}^{\Delta}
acts flag-transitively on
\mathcal{D}_{0}
. As the designs corresponding to distinct classes
\Delta,\Delta^{\prime}\in\Sigma
are isomorphic (under elements of
G
mapping
\Delta
to
\Delta^{\prime}
) we refer to
\mathcal{D}_{\Delta}
as
\mathcal{D}_{0}
.

(b)

For each block
B
of
\mathcal{D}
the set
B(\Sigma)=\{\Delta\in\Sigma:B\cap\Delta\neq\varnothing\}
has a constant size
k_{1}=\frac{k}{k_{0}}
, and the incidence structure
\mathcal{D}^{\Sigma}=\left(\Sigma,\mathcal{B}^{\Sigma},\mathcal{I}\right)
, where
\mathcal{B}^{\Sigma}
is the quotient set defined by the equivalence relation
\mathcal{R}=\{(C,C^{\prime})\in\mathcal{B}\times\mathcal{B}:C(\Sigma)=C^{\prime}(\Sigma)\}
on
\mathcal{B}
and
\mathcal{I}=\{(\Delta,C^{\Sigma})\in\Sigma\times\mathcal{B}^{\Sigma}:\Delta\in C(\Sigma)\}
, is either a
2
-
(v_{1},k_{1},\lambda_{1})
design or a symmetric
1
-design with
v_{1}
points and
k_{1}=v_{1}-1
. In both cases,
G^{\Sigma}
acts flag-transitively on
\mathcal{D}_{1}
.

Section 3 focuses on the interactions between the parameters of
\mathcal{D}
,
\mathcal{D}_{0}
and
\mathcal{D}_{1}
, and the arguments used are mostly combinatorial. In particular, in Corollary
3.10
we prove that the conclusions (1) and (2) of Theorem
1.1
hold when
\mathcal{D}
is a symmetric
2
-design. Furthermore, we provide a classification of
\mathcal{D}_{1}
in five possible families when
\mathcal{D}_{2}
is a
2
-design.

In Section 4, we firstly exclude three out of the aforementioned five families for
\mathcal{D}_{1}
. Then, denoted by
r_{1}
the replication number of
\mathcal{D}_{1}
, we prove Theorem
4.1
, which states that either
\frac{r_{1}}{(r_{1},\lambda_{1})}<\lambda
, and hence
G_{\Delta}^{\Delta}
contains large prime order automorphisms (namely automorphisms of order
\lambda
), or
\frac{r_{1}}{(r_{1},\lambda_{1})}\geqslant\lambda
,
(v_{1}-1,k_{1}-1)\leqslant(v_{1}-1)^{2}
and
(v_{1}-1)^{2}\neq 2(v_{1}-1,k_{1})-1
, and hence the socle of
G^{\Sigma}
is either elementary abelian, or non-abelian simple by a result of Zhong and Zhou
[
65
, Theorem 1.1]
.

Section 5 is dedicated to the case
\frac{r_{1}}{(r_{1},\lambda_{1})}<\lambda
and
G_{\Delta}^{\Delta}
containing large prime order automorphisms. We completely classifies the admissible
(\mathcal{D}_{0},G_{\Delta}^{\Delta})
by using
[
35
]
together with an argument that combines a result of Camina
[
11
, Lemma7]
and the classification of the groups admitting transitive permutation representation of degree a prime and that links the rank of any block stabilizer in
G_{\Delta}^{\Delta}
with the rank of
G_{\Delta}^{\Delta}
.

Section 6 provides additional meaningful restrictions on
\mathcal{D}_{0}
and
\mathcal{D}_{1}
when the action of
G
on
\Sigma
in not faithful.

In Sections 7 and 8, we adapt the group-theoretic arguments for flag-transitive linear spaces, developed by Liebeck in
[
37
]
and by Saxl in
[
48
]
, to severely restrict the possibilities for
(\mathcal{D}_{1},G^{\Sigma})
. Then we use these information with those on
(\mathcal{D}_{0},G_{\Delta}^{\Delta})
provided in Section 5 and on the kernel of the action of
G
on
\Sigma
provided in Section 6 to force
\mathcal{D}_{1}
to be a symmetric
1
-design with
k_{1}=v_{1}-1

Section 9 is the final section and is devoted to the completion of the proof of Theorem
1.1
and Corollary
1.2
. We firstly show that
\mathcal{D}_{0}
is a translation plane by matching the admissible parameters of
\mathcal{D}_{0}
with the group theoretic information provided
[
35
]
. Then, we use the classification of the flag-transitive translation plane given in
[
37
]
with the fact that
G
induces a
2
-transitive group on
\mathcal{D}_{1}
to achieve the conclusion of Theorem
1.1
. Finally, Corollary
1.2
follows from Theorem
1.1
and the results of Zieschang
[
62
]
and Zhang, Chen and Zhou
[
64
]
according as
\lambda
does not divide or does divide
r
, respectively.

1.2.
Terminology and notation.

The terminology and notation are standard and will follow that of
[
15
]
for the Design Theory, that of
[
5
,
7
,
19
,
23
,
30
,
34
,
45
]
for the Group Theory, and that of
[
15
,
27
,
28
,
29
,
38
]
for the geometric structures used throughout the paper.

2.
Preliminaries

In this section, we provide some useful results in both design theory and group theory. First, we give the following theorems which allow us to reduce the analysis of the flag-transitive automorphism group
G
of a
2
-
(v,k,\lambda)
design
\mathcal{D}
.

Lemma 2.1
.

The parameters
v
,
b
,
k
,
r
,
\lambda
of
\mathcal{D}
satisfy
vr=bk
,
\lambda(v-1)=r(k-1)
, and
k\leqslant r
.

For a proof see
[
15
, 1.3.8 and 2.1.5]
.

Theorem 2.2

(Dembowski)
.

Let
\mathcal{D}=(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{B})
be a
2
-
(v,k,\lambda)
design admitting a
flag-transitive automorphism group
G
. If
G
acts point-imprimitively on
\mathcal{D}
, then
(r,\lambda)>1
.

For a proof see
[
15
, 2.3.7(a)]
.

Theorem 2.3

(Devillers-Praeger)
.

Let
\mathcal{D}=(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{B})
be a
2
-
(v,k,\lambda)
design admitting a
flag-transitive automorphism group
G
. If
G
acts point-imprimitively on
\mathcal{D}
, then
v\leq 2\lambda^{2}(\lambda-1)
.

For a proof see
[
17
, Theorem 1]
.

Theorem 2.4

(Camina-Zieschang)
.

Let
\mathcal{D}=(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{B})
be a
2
-
(v,k,\lambda)
design admitting a
flag-transitive, point-imprimitive automorphism group
G
preserving a nontrivial
partition
\Sigma
of
\mathcal{P}
with
v_{1}
classes of size
v_{0}
. Then
v=v_{0}v_{1}
and the following hold:

(1)

There is a constant
k_{0}\geqslant 2
such that
\left|B\cap\Delta\right|=0
or
k_{0}
for each
B\in\mathcal{B}
and
\Delta\in\Sigma
. The parameter
k_{0}
divides
k
,

(2.1)

\frac{v-1}{k-1}=\frac{v_{0}-1}{k_{0}-1}\text{,}

and the following hold:

(i)

For each
\Delta\in\Sigma
, let
\mathcal{B}_{\Delta}=\left\{B\cap\Delta\neq\varnothing:B\in\mathcal{B}\right\}
. Then the set

\mathcal{R}_{\Delta}=\{(B,C)\in\mathcal{B}_{\Delta}\times\mathcal{B}_{\Delta}:B\cap\Delta=\mathrm{C}\cap\Delta\}

is an equivalence relation on
\mathcal{B}_{\Delta}
with each equivalence class of size
\mu
.

(ii)

The incidence structure
\mathcal{D}_{\Delta}=(\Delta,\mathcal{B}_{\Delta})
is either a symmetric
1
-design with
k_{0}=v_{0}-1
, or a
2
-
(v_{0},k_{0},\lambda_{0})
design with
\lambda_{0}=\frac{\lambda}{\mu}
.

(iii)

The group
G_{\Delta}^{\Delta}
acts flag-transitively on
\mathcal{D}_{\Delta}
.

(2)

For each block
B
of
\mathcal{D}
the set
B(\Sigma)=\{\Delta\in\Sigma:B\cap\Delta\neq\varnothing\}
has a constant size
k_{1}=\frac{k}{k_{0}}
. Further,

(2.2)

\frac{v_{1}-1}{k_{1}-1}=\frac{k_{0}(v_{0}-1)}{v_{0}(k_{0}-1)}

and the following hold:

(i)

The set

\mathcal{R}=\{(C,C^{\prime})\in\mathcal{B}\times\mathcal{B}:C(\Sigma)=C^{\prime}(\Sigma)\}

is an equivalence relation on
\mathcal{B}
with each class of size
\eta
;

(ii)

Let
\mathcal{B}^{\Sigma}
be the quotient set defined by
\mathcal{R}
, and for any block
C
of
\mathcal{D}
denote by
C^{\Sigma}
the
\mathcal{R}
-equivalence class containing
C
. Then the incidence structure
\mathcal{D}^{\Sigma}=\left(\Sigma,\mathcal{B}^{\Sigma},\mathcal{I}\right)
with
\mathcal{I}=\{(\Delta,C^{\Sigma})\in\Sigma\times\mathcal{B}^{\Sigma}:\Delta\in C(\Sigma)\}
is either a symmetric
1
-design with
k_{1}=v_{1}-1
, or a
2
-
(v_{1},k_{1},\lambda_{1})
design with
\lambda_{1}=\frac{v_{0}^{2}\lambda}{k_{0}^{2}\eta}
.

(iii)

The group
G^{\Sigma}
acts flag-transitively on
\mathcal{D}^{\Sigma}
.

For a proof see
[
12
, Propositions 2.1 and 2.3]
.

We refer to
\mathcal{D}^{\Sigma}
simply as
\mathcal{D}_{1}
. Moreover, as mentioned in the introduction, the designs corresponding to distinct classes
\Delta,\Delta^{\prime}\in\Sigma
are isomorphic under elements of
G
mapping
\Delta
to
\Delta^{\prime}
, we refer to
\mathcal{D}_{\Delta}
as
\mathcal{D}_{0}
. The parameters of
\mathcal{D}_{0}
and of
\mathcal{D}_{1}
will be indexed by
0
and
1
, respectively. Hence, the conclusions of Lemma
2.1
hold for
\mathcal{D}_{i}
when this one is a
2
-
(v_{i},k_{i},\lambda_{i})
design. That is,
v_{i}r_{i}=b_{i}k_{i}
,
\lambda(v_{i}-1)=r_{i}(k-1)
, and
k_{i}\leqslant r_{i}
, where
r_{i}
and
b_{i}
are the replication number and the number of blocks of
\mathcal{D}_{i}
, respectively.

Lemma 2.5
.

If
\mathcal{D}_{i}
is a
2
-
(v_{i},k_{i},\lambda_{i})
design,
i=0,1
, then the following hold:

(1)

r_{i}>\lambda_{i}^{1/2}v_{i}^{1/2}
;

(2)

\frac{r_{i}}{(r_{i},\lambda_{i})}\mid(e_{i1},...,e_{im_{i}},v_{i}-1)
, where the
e_{0j}
’s are the lengths of the point-
G_{x}^{\Delta}
-orbits on
\mathcal{D}_{0}
distinct from
\{x\}
, and the
e_{1j}
’s are the lengths of the point-
G_{\Delta}^{\Sigma}
-orbits on
\mathcal{D}_{1}
distinct from
\{\Delta\}
.

Proof.

Since
r_{i}\geqslant k_{i}
, it follows that
r_{i}^{2}>r_{i}k_{i}-r_{i}+\lambda_{i}=\lambda_{i}v_{i}
and hence (1) holds true. Finally, for any
x,y\in\Delta
with
x\neq y
and any
B
block of
\mathcal{D}_{0}
containing
x
, the incidence structure
(y^{G_{x}^{\Delta}},B^{G_{x}^{\Delta}})
is a
1
-design by
[
15
, 1.2.6]
, (2) follows. The same conclusions hold for the incidence structure
((\Delta^{\prime})^{G_{\Delta}^{\Sigma}},(B^{\Sigma})^{G_{\Delta}^{\Sigma}},\mathcal{I}^{\prime})
with
\Delta,\Delta^{\prime}\in\Sigma
and
\Delta\neq\Delta^{\prime}
,
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
the incidence relation inherited by
\mathcal{I}
, and
B^{\Sigma}
any block of
\mathcal{D}_{1}
incident to
\Delta
with respect to
\mathcal{I}
.
∎

As we will see, an important parameter in the analysis of
\mathcal{D}
is the greatest common divisor
\left(k_{1}-1,v_{1}-1\right)
, which will simply denoted by
A
throughout the paper.

2.1.
Minimal partition

Assume that
G
preserves a further non-trivial partition
\Sigma^{\prime}
of the point-set of
\mathcal{D}
in
v_{1}^{\prime}
classes of size
v_{0}^{\prime}
. We may apply Theorem
2.4
to the triple
(\mathcal{D},G,\Sigma^{\prime})
deriving information on the incidence structures
\mathcal{D}_{0}^{\prime}
and
\mathcal{D}_{1}^{\prime}
which have the same meaning as
\mathcal{D}_{0}
and
\mathcal{D}_{1}
, respectively, but they are related to the partition
\Sigma^{\prime}
. In this setting, we say that
\Sigma^{\prime}

refines

\Sigma
, or that
\Sigma^{\prime}
is
finer
than
\Sigma
, and write
\Sigma^{\prime}\preceq\Sigma
, if any element of
\Sigma^{\prime}
is contained in a (unique) element of
\Sigma^{\prime}
. The binary relation ’
\preceq
’ on the set of the
G
-invariant non-trivial partitions of the point-set of
\mathcal{D}
is a partial ordering. Therefore, it makes sense to call any such partition
minimal
if it is minimal with respect to ordering ’
\preceq
’. Clearly, for any given non-trivial
G
-invariant partition
\Sigma
of the point-set of
\mathcal{D}
, there always is a minimal non-trivial
G
-invariant partition
\Sigma^{\prime}
of the point-set of
\mathcal{D}
refining
\Sigma
.

Based on Theorems
2.2
,
2.3
and
2.4
, and on the previous remark, we make the following hypothesis.

Hypothesis 2.6
.

\mathcal{D}=(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{B})
is a
2
-
(v,k,\lambda)
design with
\lambda
a prime divisor of
r
such that
v\leqslant 2\lambda^{2}(\lambda-1)
, and
G
is a flag-transitive, point-imprimitive automorphism group of
\mathcal{D}
preserving a minimal nontrivial
partition
\Sigma
of
\mathcal{P}
with
v_{1}
classes of size
v_{0}
.

Lemma 2.7
.

Assume that Hypothesis
2.6
holds. Then the following hold

(1)

k-1\mid v-1
and
k_{0}-1\mid v_{0}-1
;

(2)

If
\mathcal{D}_{0}
is a
2
-design, then either
\mu=\lambda
,
\lambda_{0}=1
and
\mathcal{D}_{0}
is a linear space, or
\mu=1
,
\lambda_{0}=\lambda
,
\mathcal{D}_{0}
is a
2
-
(v_{0},k_{0},\lambda)
design and
r=r_{0}=\frac{v_{0}-1}{k_{0}-1}\lambda
;

(3)

G_{\Delta}^{\Delta}
acts point-primitively on
\mathcal{D}_{0}

(4)

If
\mathcal{D}_{1}
is a
2
-design, then

(2.3)

r_{1}=\frac{(v_{1}-1)\lambda_{1}}{k_{1}-1}=\frac{v_{0}(v_{0}-1)\lambda}{k_{0}(k_{0}-1)\eta}\quad\text{ and }\quad b_{1}=\frac{v(v_{0}-1)\lambda}{k(k_{0}-1)\eta}\text{,}

Proof.

Assertion (1) immediately follows from (
2.1
) in Theorem
2.4
(1) since
\lambda\mid r
by Hypothesis
2.6
. Assertion (2) follows from (1) and Theorem
2.4
(1.ii) since
\mu\mid\lambda
and
\lambda
is prime.

Assume to the contrary that
G_{\Delta}^{\Delta}
does not act point-primitively on
\mathcal{D}_{0}
. Then there is a subgroup a subgroup
M
of
G_{\Delta}
containing
G_{x}
, where
x\in\Delta
Let
\Delta\in\Sigma
. Then
\Sigma^{\prime}=\{x^{Mg}:g\in G\}
is a non-trivial
G
-invariant of the point-set of
\mathcal{D}
refining
\Sigma
by
[
19
, Theorem 1.5A]
, which is contrary to the minimality of
\Sigma
. Thus, assertion (3) holds true.

Finally, assertion (4) follows from Theorem
2.4
(2).
∎

It is worthwhile noting that, the minimality of
\Sigma
in the Hypothesis
2.6
is only needed when
k_{0}\geqslant 3
and
\mu=1
. Indeed, the minimality of
\Sigma
is equivalent to the fact that
G_{\Delta}^{\Delta}
acts point-primitively on
\mathcal{D}_{0}
by
[
19
, Theorem 1.5A]
. Now,
G_{\Delta}^{\Delta}
acts point-
2
-transitively on
\mathcal{D}_{0}
, an hence
G_{\Delta}^{\Delta}
acts point-primitively on
\mathcal{D}_{0}
, when
k_{0}=2
; the same conclusion holds when
k_{0}\geqslant 3
and
\mu=\lambda
by
[
26
, Theorem 8]
.

It results that,
\eta\mid\frac{v_{0}}{k_{0}}\lambda
by Lemma
2.7
(4) since
\lambda_{1}=\frac{v_{0}^{2}\lambda}{k_{0}^{2}\eta}
by Theorem
2.4
(2.ii). Set
\eta_{0}=\left(\eta,\frac{v_{0}}{k_{0}}\right)
,
\eta_{1}=\frac{\eta}{\eta_{0}}
and
r_{1}^{\prime}=\frac{v_{0}}{k_{0}\eta_{0}}\cdot\frac{v_{0}-1}{k_{0}-1}
. Then

(2.4)

r_{1}=r_{1}^{\prime}\cdot\frac{\lambda}{\eta_{1}}

by (
2.3
).
The symbols
\eta_{0}
,
\eta_{1}
and
r_{1}^{\prime}
will have these fixed meanings throughout the paper.

3.
Combinatorial reductions

This section focuses on the interactions between the parameters of
\mathcal{D}
,
\mathcal{D}_{0}
and
\mathcal{D}_{1}
. The argument are mostly combinatorial and fully exploit the fact that
\lambda
is a prime number. In particular, we prove that the conclusions of Theorem
1.1
hold when
\mathcal{D}
is a symmetric
2
-design. Furthermore, when
\mathcal{D}_{2}
is a
2
-design we provide a classification of
\mathcal{D}_{1}
in five possible families. These families are excluded in the next sections.

Lemma 3.1
.

Assume that Hypothesis
2.6
holds. Then
2\leqslant k_{0}<v_{0}
and
v_{0}\neq 3
.

Proof.

Suppose that
k_{0}=v_{0}
. Then
v=k
by (
2.1
) in Theorem
2.4
, a contradiction. Thus
2\leqslant k_{0}<v_{0}
, hence
v_{0}\geqslant 3
.

Assume that
v_{0}=3
. Then
k_{0}=2
, and hence
v=2(k-1)+1=2k-1
and
r=2\lambda
by (
2.1
) in Theorem
2.4
(1). Thus
k\mid r
since
k\mid vr
, and hence
k\mid 2\lambda
. If
\mathcal{D}_{1}

is a
2
-design, then
\lambda_{1}=\frac{\left(v_{0}\right)^{2}\lambda}{\left(k_{0}\right)^{2}\eta}=\frac{9\lambda}{4\eta}
is an integer by Theorem
2.4
(2.ii), and
hence
4\mid\lambda
, which is not the case since
\lambda
is a prime.
Thus
\mathcal{D}_{1}
is a symmetric
1
-design with
k_{1}=v_{1}-1
again by Theorem
2.4
(2.ii). Then
k/2+1=v/3
since
k_{1}=k/2
and
v_{1}=v/3
, and so
v=3\frac{k+2}{2}
. Therefore
3\frac{k+2}{2}=2k-1
since
v=2k-1
,
and hence
k=8
. Then
4\mid\lambda
since
k\mid 2\lambda
, a contradiction. Thus,
v_{0}\neq 3
.
∎

Lemma 3.2
.

Assume that Hypothesis
2.6
holds. Then
\mathcal{D}_{0}
is a
2
-design.

Proof.

Suppose the contrary. Then
\mathcal{D}_{0}
is a symmetric
1
-design and
k_{0}=v_{0}-1
by Theorem
2.4
(1.ii). Since
\frac{r}{\lambda}
is an integer and

\frac{r}{\lambda}=\frac{v-1}{k-1}=\frac{v_{0}-1}{k_{0}-1}=\frac{v_{0}-1}{v_{0}-2}

by (
2.1
) in Theorem
2.4
(1), it follows that
v_{0}-2=1
. So
v_{0}=3
, contrary to Lemma
3.1
.
∎

Lemma 3.3
.

Assume that Hypothesis
2.6
holds. If
\mathcal{D}_{1}
is a symmetric
1
-design with
k_{1}=v_{1}-1
, then the parameters of
\mathcal{D}_{0}
,
\mathcal{D}_{1}
,
\mathcal{D}
are as in Table
1
. In particular,
\lambda\geqslant k_{0}
, and
\lambda=k_{0}
if and only if
\mathcal{D}
is a symmetric
2
-design.

Table 1
.
Admissible parameters for
\mathcal{D}_{0}
,
\mathcal{D}_{1}
,
\mathcal{D}
for
k_{1}=v_{1}-1

v_{0}

k_{0}

\lambda_{0}

r_{0}

b_{0}

v_{1}

k_{1}

v

k

\lambda

r

b

k_{0}^{2}

k_{0}

\frac{\lambda}{\mu}

(k_{0}+1)\frac{\lambda}{\mu}

k_{0}(k_{0}+1)\frac{\lambda}{\mu}

k_{0}+2

k_{0}+1

k_{0}^{2}(k_{0}+2)

k_{0}(k_{0}+1)

\lambda

(k_{0}+1)\lambda

k_{0}(k_{0}+2)\lambda

Proof.

Assume that
\mathcal{D}_{1}
is a symmetric
1
-design with
k_{1}=v_{1}-1
. Then
k=k_{0}(v_{1}-1)
. Moreover,

(3.1)

v_{0}\left(v_{1}-1\right)=\frac{v_{0}-1}{k_{0}-1}\left(v_{1}-2\right)k_{0}

by (
2.2
) in Theorem
2.4
(2.ii), which implies
v_{1}-2\mid v_{0}
and
v_{1}-1\mid\frac{v_{0}-1}{k_{0}-1}k_{0}
.
Then
v_{0}=\theta(v_{1}-2)
and
\frac{v_{0}-1}{k_{0}-1}k_{0}=\rho(v_{1}-1)
for
some positive integers
\theta
and
\rho
, respectively. These equalities, substituted in (
3.1
), yield
\theta=\rho
. Therefore,

(3.2)

v_{0}=\theta(v_{1}-2)\text{ \quad and \quad}\frac{v_{0}-1}{k_{0}-1}k_{0}=\theta(v_{1}-1)\text{.}

Now substituting the value of
v_{0}
in the second equality of (
3.2
), one obtains

\left(\theta(v_{1}-2)-1\right)k_{0}=\theta(v_{1}-1)\left(k_{0}-1\right)=\theta(v_{1}-1)k_{0}-\theta(v_{1}-1)

Easy computations lead to
k_{0}=\theta\frac{v_{1}-1}{\theta+1}
, and hence
\theta=\left(v_{0},k_{0}\right)

since
v_{0}=\theta(v_{1}-2)
. Therefore, one has

(3.3)

r=\frac{\theta(v_{1}-2)-1}{\theta\frac{v_{1}-1}{\theta+1}-1}\lambda=\lambda\left(\theta+1\right)\text{.}

Now,
k\mid vr
implies
\theta\frac{v_{1}-1}{\theta+1}\left(v_{1}-1\right)\mid\theta(v_{1}-2)v_{1}\lambda\left(\theta+1\right)
, from which we derive

(3.4)

\left(\frac{v_{1}-1}{\theta+1}\right)^{2}\mid v_{1}(v_{1}-2)\lambda\text{\quad and hence \quad}\left(\frac{v_{1}-1}{\theta+1}\right)^{2}\mid\lambda

Then
\frac{v_{1}-1}{\theta+1}=1
since
\lambda
is a prime number.
Then
\theta=v_{1}-2
. Therefore
v_{0}=(v_{1}-2)^{2}
,
k_{0}=v_{1}-2
, and hence Table
1
holds. Moreover,
\lambda\geqslant k_{0}
since
k\leqslant r
, and
\lambda=k_{0}
if and only if
\mathcal{D}
is a symmetric
2
-design.
∎

From now on, unless stated otherwise, we assume
\mathcal{D}_{1}
is not a symmetric
1
-design with
k_{1}=v_{1}-1
. Hence,
\mathcal{D}_{1}
is a
2
-design by Theorem
2.4
. The remaining case, namely the case where
\mathcal{D}_{1}
is a symmetric
1
-design with
k_{1}=v_{1}-1
, is settled in the last section of the paper. Hence, from now on we make the following hypothesis.

Hypothesis 3.4
.

Hypothesis
2.6
holds,
\mathcal{D}_{1}
is not a symmetric
1
-design with
k_{1}=v_{1}-1
.

For a given positive integer
n
and a prime divisor
p
of
n
, we denote the
p
-part
of
n
by
n_{p}
, that is to say,
n_{p}=p^{t}
with
p^{t}\mid n
but
p^{t+1}\nmid n
. In this case,
t
is called the
p
-adic value
of
n
and is denoted by
v_{p}(n)
.

Proposition 3.5
.

Assume that Hypothesis
3.4
holds. Then then the following hold:

(1)

v_{0}=\left(z(k_{0}-1)+1\right)k_{0}
with
z\geqslant 1
;

(2)

\frac{v_{0}-1}{k_{0}-1}=zk_{0}+1
;

(3)

k_{1}=A\left(z(k_{0}-1)+1\right)+1
and
v_{1}=A\left(zk_{0}+1\right)+1
.

(4)

\lambda\geqslant\left(k_{0}-1\right)A+1
. In particular, either
\lambda>k_{0}
, or
\lambda=k_{0}
and
A=1
.

Proof.

Suppose that
k_{0}
does not divide
v_{0}
. Then
\frac{k_{0}^{2}}{(k_{0}^{2},v_{0}^{2})}>1
, and hence
\frac{k_{0}^{2}}{(k_{0}^{2},v_{0}^{2})}=\lambda
since
\mathcal{D}_{1}
is a
2
-design with
\lambda_{1}=\frac{v_{0}^{2}\lambda}{k_{0}^{2}\eta}
. Thus,
v_{\lambda}\left(\frac{k_{0}^{2}}{(k_{0}^{2},v_{0}^{2})}\right)=1
.

Let
i=v_{\lambda}(k_{0})
, then
i\geqslant 1
,
v_{\lambda}(k_{0}^{2})=2i
and
v_{\lambda}(k_{0}^{2}/\lambda)=2i-1
. Further,
v_{\lambda}(v_{0}^{2})\geqslant 2i-1
since
k_{0}^{2}/\lambda=(k_{0}^{2},v_{0}^{2})
divides
v_{0}^{2}
. Actually,
v_{\lambda}(v_{0}^{2})\geqslant 2i
since
v_{\lambda}(v_{0}^{2})=2\cdot v_{\lambda}(v_{0})
. Therefore
v_{\lambda}\left((k_{0}^{2},v_{0}^{2})\right)=2i
since
v_{\lambda}(k_{0}^{2})=2i
, and hence

1=v_{\lambda}\left(\frac{k_{0}^{2}}{(k_{0}^{2},v_{0}^{2})}\right)=v_{\lambda}(k_{0}^{2})-v_{\lambda}\left((k_{0}^{2},v_{0}^{2})\right)=2i-2i=0\text{,}

a contradiction. Thus
k_{0}
divides
v_{0}
, and hence
v_{0}=\theta k_{0}
for some
\theta\geqslant 1
.

We know that
k_{0}-1\mid v_{0}-1
by Lemma
2.7
(1), hence

\frac{v_{0}-1}{k_{0}-1}=\frac{\theta k_{0}-1}{k_{0}-1}=\frac{\theta k_{0}-\theta+\theta-1}{k_{0}-1}=\theta+\frac{\theta-1}{k_{0}-1}\text{.}

Therefore,
\theta=z(k_{0}-1)+1
for some positive integer
z
. Thus

v_{0}=\left(z(k_{0}-1)+1\right)k_{0}

with
z\geqslant 1
, which is (1), and

\frac{v_{0}-1}{k_{0}-1}=\left(z(k_{0}-1)+1\right)+z=zk_{0}+1\text{,}

which is (2).

Now (1), Lemma
2.7
(1), (
2.2
) in Theorem
2.4
(2) imply

\frac{v_{0}}{k_{0}}\left(v_{1}-1\right)=\frac{v_{0}-1}{k_{0}-1}\left(k_{1}-1\right)

and hence
\frac{v_{0}}{k_{0}}\mid k_{1}-1
and
\frac{v_{0}-1}{k_{0}-1}\mid v_{1}-1
. Thus, there are positive integers
x
and
y
such that

x\frac{v_{0}}{k_{0}}=k_{1}-1\text{ and }y\frac{v_{0}-1}{k_{0}-1}=v_{1}-1

Therefore

\frac{v_{0}}{k_{0}}y\frac{v_{0}-1}{k_{0}-1}=\frac{v_{0}-1}{k_{0}-1}x\frac{v_{0}}{k_{0}}\text{,}

and hence
y=x
. So,
k_{1}-1=A\frac{v_{0}}{k_{0}}
and
v_{1}-1=A\frac{v_{0}-1}{k_{0}-1}
since
A=\left(k_{1}-1,v_{1}-1\right)
and
\left(\frac{v_{0}}{k_{0}},\frac{v_{0}-1}{k_{0}-1}\right)=1
. Thus

k_{1}=A\left(z(k_{0}-1)+1\right)+1\text{ and }v_{1}=A\left(zk_{0}+1\right)+1\text{,}

by (1) and (2), and we obtain (3).

Now
r^{2}>\lambda v
with
r=\frac{v_{0}-1}{k_{0}-1}\lambda
by Lemma
2.7
(2) since
\mathcal{D}_{0}
is a
2
-design by Lemma
3.2
. Moreover,
v=v_{0}v_{1}
and (2) holds. All these facts imply

\left(\frac{v_{0}-1}{k_{0}-1}\right)^{2}\lambda^{2}>\lambda v_{0}v_{1}=\lambda v_{0}\left(A\frac{v_{0}-1}{k_{0}-1}+1\right)\text{,}

and hence

(3.5)

\left(\frac{v_{0}-1}{k_{0}-1}\right)^{2}\lambda>\left(v_{0}-1\right)\left(A\frac{v_{0}-1}{k_{0}-1}+1\right)+A\frac{v_{0}-1}{k_{0}-1}+1\text{.}

Dividing (
3.5
) by
\frac{v_{0}-1}{k_{0}-1}
, one obtains

(3.6)

\left(\frac{v_{0}-1}{k_{0}-1}\right)\lambda>\left(k_{0}-1\right)\left(A\frac{v_{0}-1}{k_{0}-1}+1\right)+A\text{.}

Finally, dividing (
3.6
) once again by
\frac{v_{0}-1}{k_{0}-1}
, (4) follows.
∎

Remark 3.6
.

The following equalities can be deduced from the proof of Proposition
3.5
(3):

(3.7)

v_{1}=A\frac{v_{0}-1}{k_{0}-1}+1\text{\quad and \quad}k_{1}=A\frac{v_{0}}{k_{0}}+1\text{.}

In the sequel, we will use the equalities in (
3.7
) without recalling them all the times.

Lemma 3.7
.

Assume that Hypothesis
3.4
holds. Then the following hold:

(1)

\left(k_{1},v_{1}\right)=(z,A+1)
;

(2)

(k_{1},r_{1}^{\prime})=\left(Az-1,zk_{0}+1\right)=\left(Az-1,A+k_{0}\right)
;

(3)

\left(k_{1},v_{1},r_{1}^{\prime}\right)=1
and
\left(k_{1},v_{1}\right)\cdot(k_{1},r_{1}^{\prime})\mid k_{1}
;

(4)

One of the following holds:

(i)

k_{1}=\left(k_{1},v_{1}\right)\cdot\left(k_{1},\frac{r}{\lambda}\right)\cdot\lambda
,
\eta_{1}=1
,
\eta_{0}=\eta
,
\eta\mid\frac{v_{0}}{k_{0}}
and
r_{1}^{\prime}=\frac{r_{1}}{\lambda}
;

(ii)

k_{1}=\left(k_{1},v_{1}\right)\cdot\left(k_{1},r_{1}\right)
,
v_{0}
is even and

(3.8)

\left(k_{0},k_{1},v_{1},r_{1}\right)=\left(2,\frac{1}{2}(v_{0}-2)(v_{0}-1),(v_{0}-2)^{2},\frac{v_{0}}{2\eta_{0}}(v_{0}-1)\frac{\lambda}{\eta_{1}}\right)\text{.}

Further, if
\eta_{1}=\lambda
then
\eta_{0}=1
.

Proof.

By using Proposition
3.5
(2)–(3), it is easy to see that

\displaystyle\left(k_{1},v_{1}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(A\left(zk_{0}+1-z\right)+1,A\left(zk_{0}+1\right)+1\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(A\left(zk_{0}+1\right)+1-Az,A\left(zk_{0}+1\right)+1\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(Az,A\left(zk_{0}+1\right)+1\right)=\left(Az,A+1\right)=(z,A+1)

and

\displaystyle(k_{1},r_{1}^{\prime})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(A\frac{v_{0}}{k_{0}}+1,\frac{v_{0}}{k_{0}\eta_{0}}\cdot\frac{v_{0}-1}{k_{0}-1}\right)=\left(A\frac{v_{0}}{k_{0}}+1,\frac{v_{0}-1}{k_{0}-1}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(A\left(zk_{0}+1\right)+1-Az,zk_{0}+1\right)=\left(Az-1,zk_{0}+1\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(Az-1,Az+zk_{0}\right)=\left(Az-1,A+k_{0}\right)\text{,}

which proves (1) and (2). Further, from (1) and since
(k_{1},r_{1}^{\prime})=\left(Az-1,zk_{0}+1\right)
in (2), it follows that
\left(k_{1},v_{1},r_{1}^{\prime}\right)=1
. Hence
\left(k_{1},v_{1}\right)\cdot(k_{1},r_{1}^{\prime})\mid k_{1}
, which is (3).

Now,
k_{1}\mid v_{1}\cdot r_{1}
implies
k_{1}\mid\left(k_{1},v_{1}\right)\cdot\left(k_{1},r_{1}^{\prime}\right)\cdot\left(k_{1},\frac{\lambda}{\eta_{1}}\right)
. On the other hand,
\left(k_{1},v_{1}\right)\cdot(k_{1},r_{1}^{\prime})\mid k_{1}
by (3). Thus, either

(3.9)

k_{1}=\left(k_{1},v_{1}\right)\cdot\left(k_{1},r_{1}^{\prime}\right)\cdot\lambda\text{ and }\eta_{1}=1\text{, \quad or \quad}k_{1}=\left(k_{1},v_{1}\right)\cdot\left(k_{1},r_{1}^{\prime}\right)

since
\lambda
is a prime. The former leads to (4.i). Hence, assume that
k_{1}=\left(k_{1},v_{1}\right)\cdot\left(k_{1},r_{1}^{\prime}\right)
. Then (1), (2) and Proposition
3.5
(3) lead to

(3.10)

A\left(z\left(k_{0}-1\right)+1\right)+1=(z,A+1)\cdot\left(Az-1,A+k_{0}\right)\text{.}

If either
(z,A+1)\leqslant\frac{z}{2}
or
\left(Az-1,A+k_{0}\right)\leqslant\frac{A+k_{0}}{2}
, then (
3.10
) implies

A\left(z\left(k_{0}-1\right)+1\right)+1\leqslant\frac{z(A+k_{0})}{2}

from which we derive

(3.11)

A\leqslant\frac{zk_{0}-2}{\left(2zk_{0}-3z+2\right)}<\frac{zk_{0}}{\left(2zk_{0}-3z\right)}=\frac{k_{0}}{2k_{0}-3}

since
k_{0}\geqslant 2
. Since
A<1
for
k_{0}\geqslant 3
, it follows that
k_{0}=2
and
A\leqslant\frac{2(z-1)}{z+2}<2
. Thus,
A=1
and
z\geqslant 4
. On the other hand, (
3.10
) becomes
z+2=(z,2)\cdot(z-1,3)
and
z\leqslant 4
. Therefore
z=4
, and hence
(v_{0},k_{0},v_{1},k_{1})=(10,2,10,6)
by Proposition
3.5
(1)(3). Moreover,
G^{\Sigma}
acts point-
2
-transitively on
\mathcal{D}_{1}
by
[
31
, Corollary 4.6]
, and hence either
PSL_{2}(9)\unlhd G^{\Sigma}\leqslant P\Gamma L_{2}(9)
or
A_{10}\unlhd G^{\Sigma}\leqslant S_{10}
by
[
19
, Table B.4]
. The latter implies
b_{1}=\binom{10}{6}
since
G^{\Sigma}
acts point-
6
-transitively on
\mathcal{D}_{1}
. Then
r_{1}=126
since
b_{1}k_{1}=v_{1}r_{1}
and hence
\lambda_{1}=70
since
r_{1}=\frac{(v_{1}-1)\lambda_{1}}{k_{1}-1}
. Therefore
70=5\cdot\frac{5\lambda}{\eta}
by Theorem
2.4
(2.ii), and hence
14\mid\lambda
, whereas
\lambda
is a prime. Thus
PSL_{2}(9)\unlhd G^{\Sigma}\leqslant P\Gamma L_{2}(9)
, and hence
G_{\Delta}^{\Sigma}
, with
\Delta\in\Sigma
, is a
\{2,3\}
-group. Now,
r_{1}=\frac{9\lambda_{1}}{5}
is an integer dividing the order of
G_{\Delta}^{\Sigma}
since
G^{\Sigma}
acts flag-transitively on
\mathcal{D}_{1}
. If
r_{1}
is even then
\lambda_{1}
is even, and hence
\lambda=2
since
\lambda_{1}=5\cdot\frac{5\lambda}{\eta}
by Theorem
2.4
(2.ii), but this is contrary to Theorem
2.3
. Therefore,
r_{1}
is odd, and hence
r_{1}=9
and
\lambda_{1}=5
since
r_{1}=\frac{9\lambda_{1}}{5}
and
PSL_{2}(9)\unlhd G^{\Sigma}\leqslant P\Gamma L_{2}(9)
. So
\mathcal{D}_{1}
is a
2
-
(10,6,5)
design admitting
A_{6}\unlhd G^{\Sigma}
since
PSL_{2}(9)\cong A_{6}
, but this is contrary to
[
64
, Theorem 1]
. Thus
(z,A+1)=z
and
\left(Az-1,A+k_{0}\right)=A+k_{0}
, and hence (
3.10
) becomes

(3.12)

A\left(z\left(k_{0}-1\right)+1\right)+1=z\cdot\left(A+k_{0}\right)\text{,}

and so

(3.13)

k_{0}(A-1)z=(2z-1)A-1\text{.}

Assume that
A>1
. Then (
3.13
) implies

(3.14)

k_{0}=\frac{(2z-1)A-1}{(A-1)z}<\frac{2zA}{(A-1)z}\leqslant 4\text{,}

forcing
k_{0}=2
or
3
.

Suppose that
k_{0}=3
. Then
z(3-A)=A+1
by (
3.13
), and hence
A=2
and
z=3
since
z\geqslant 0
and
A>1
. Thus
v_{0}=v_{1}=21
,
k_{1}=15
and hence
r_{1}=70\cdot\frac{7\lambda}{\eta}
with
\eta\mid 7\lambda
. Moreover,
G^{\Sigma}
acts either point-
2
-transitively or as a point-primitive group with subdegrees
1,10,10
on
\mathcal{D}_{1}
by
[
31
, Corollary 4.6]
. Actually,
A_{21}\unlhd G^{\Sigma}\leqslant S_{21}
by
[
19
, Table B.4]
and
[
13
]
since
70
divides
r_{1}
and hence the order of
G_{\Delta}^{\Sigma}
with
\Delta\in\Sigma
. Then
b_{1}=\binom{21}{15}
since
G^{\Sigma}
acts point-
15
-transitively on
\mathcal{D}_{1}
, and so
r_{1}=54264
since
b_{1}k_{1}=v_{1}r_{1}
. Then
r_{1}
is not divisible by
7
, whereas
r_{1}=70\cdot\frac{7\lambda}{\eta}
with
\eta\mid 7\lambda
, a contradiction.

Suppose that that
k_{0}=2
. Then
A=2z-1=v_{0}-3
by (
3.13
), and hence

(3.15)

v_{1}=(v_{0}-2)^{2}\text{ \quad and \quad}k_{1}=\frac{1}{2}(v_{0}-2)(v_{0}-1)\text{ \quad and \quad}r_{1}=\frac{v_{0}}{2\eta_{0}}(v_{0}-1)\frac{\lambda}{\eta_{1}}\text{,}

If
\eta_{1}=1
, then
\eta_{0}=1
since
k_{1}\leqslant r_{1}
,
v_{0}>3
by Lemma
3.1
and
v_{0}
even, and we obtain the the last part of (4.ii).

Finally, assume that
A=1
. Then
z=1
by (
3.13
),
v_{0}
is a square,
k_{0}=\sqrt{v_{0}}
,
v_{1}=\sqrt{v_{0}}+2
and
k_{1}=\sqrt{v_{0}}+1
by Proposition
3.5
(2)–(3). Then
\mathcal{D}_{1}
is a symmetric
2
-designs, and hence
\lambda_{1}=\sqrt{v_{0}}
. In particular,
\mathcal{D}_{1}
is a symmetric
1
-designs with
k_{1}=v_{1}-1
, but this contradicts our assumption.
∎

Proposition 3.8
.

Assume that Hypothesis
3.4
holds. If
\mathcal{D}_{1}
is a
2
-design with
k_{1}=\left(k_{1},v_{1}\right)\cdot\left(k_{1},\frac{r}{\lambda}\right)\cdot\lambda
, then one of the following holds:

(1)

\lambda>k_{0}
,
\lambda
divides both
v_{0}-1
and
v_{1}-1
,
\lambda\equiv 1\pmod{4}
and

(3.16)

\left(k_{0},v_{0},k_{1},v_{1}\right)=\left(2,\frac{1}{2}\left(\lambda^{2}+\lambda+2\right),\frac{1}{4}\lambda^{2}\left(\lambda-1\right),\frac{1}{2}\left(\lambda-1\right)\left(\lambda^{2}-2\right)\right)\text{;}

(2)

\lambda
divides
v_{0}
and

(3.17)

\left(k_{0},v_{0},k_{1},v_{1}\right)=\left(\lambda,\lambda\left(\lambda^{2}+\lambda-1\right),\lambda\left(\lambda+1\right),\lambda^{2}+2\lambda+2\right)\text{;}

(3)

\lambda>k_{0}
and
\lambda
does not divide any of the integers
v_{0}(v_{0}-1)
,
v_{1}(v_{1}-1)
or
v(v-1)
.

Proof.

Assume that
\lambda\mid v_{1}(v_{1}-1)
. If
\lambda\mid v_{1}
, then
\lambda\mid(k_{1},v_{1})
since
\lambda\mid k_{1}
, and so
\lambda\mid(z,A+1)
by Lemma
3.7
(1). On the other hand,
\lambda\geqslant(k_{0}-1)A+1
by Proposition
3.5
(4). Hence,
k_{0}=2
and
\lambda=A+1
divides
z
. Then there is a positive integer
w
such
z=w\lambda
, and hence

k_{1}=A\left(z+1\right)+1=(\lambda-1)(w\lambda+1)+1=w\lambda^{2}-\lambda(w-1)\text{.}

Since
k_{1}=\left(k_{1},v_{1}\right)\cdot\left(k_{1},\frac{r}{\lambda}\right)\cdot\lambda
with
\lambda\mid(k_{1},v_{1})
, it follows that
\lambda^{2}\mid k_{1}
and hence
w=a\lambda+1
for some positive integer
a
. So
v\geqslant k=2\lambda^{2}(a\lambda-a+1)\geqslant 2\lambda^{3}
, but this contradicts the Hypothesis
3.4
. Thus
\lambda\mid v_{1}-1
, and hence
\lambda\mid v_{0}-1
by (
2.2
) in Theorem
2.4
(2) since
\lambda\nmid k_{1}-1
. Then
\lambda\mid A\left(zk_{0}+1\right)
since
v_{1}=A\left(zk_{0}+1\right)+1
by Proposition
3.5
(3), and hence
\lambda\mid zk_{0}+1
since
\lambda\geqslant A+1
. Then
\lambda\mid r_{1}^{\prime}
since
r_{1}^{\prime}=\frac{v_{0}}{k_{0}\eta_{0}}\cdot\frac{v_{0}-1}{k_{0}-1}
and
\frac{v_{0}-1}{k_{0}-1}=zk_{0}+1
by Proposition
3.5
(2), and hence
\lambda\mid(Az-1,A+k_{0})
by Lemma
3.7
(2). In particular,
\lambda^{2}\mid k_{1}
since
k_{1}=\left(k_{1},v_{1}\right)\cdot\left(k_{1},\frac{r}{\lambda}\right)\cdot\lambda
and
r^{\prime}=r/\lambda
. Combining
\lambda\mid A+k_{0}
with Proposition
3.5
(4), it follows that

(3.18)

(k_{0}-1)A+1\leqslant\lambda\leqslant A+k_{0}\text{.}

Suppose that
k_{0}\geqslant 3
, then
A\leqslant\frac{k_{0}-1}{k_{0}-2}
. Hence, either
k_{0}=3
and
A\leqslant 2
, or
k_{0}\geqslant 4
and
A=1
. Assume that the latter occurs. Then
k_{1}=z(k_{0}-1)+2
and
k_{0}\leqslant\lambda\leqslant k_{0}+1
. Actually,
\lambda=k_{0}+1
since
\lambda
divides
\left(k_{1},\frac{r}{\lambda}\right)=(z-1,k_{0}+1)
, and hence
k_{1}=(e\lambda+1)(\lambda-2)+2
for some positive integer
e
such that
\lambda\mid 2e-1
since
\lambda^{2}\mid k_{1}
. Then

\left[\left(\frac{\lambda+1}{2}\lambda+1\right)(\lambda-2)+2\right](\lambda-1)\leqslant k\leqslant v\leqslant 2\lambda^{2}(\lambda-1)

by Hypothesis
3.4
, and hence
(\lambda,e)=(5,3)
since
\lambda=k_{0}+1
and
k_{0}\geqslant 4
. So
(k_{0},v_{0},k_{1},v_{1})=(4,196,50,66)
and
A=1
, hence
A_{196}\unlhd G_{\Delta}^{\Delta}\leqslant S_{196}
by
[
31
, Corollary 4.2]
and
[
19
, Table B.4]
, contrary to
[
18
, Proposition 2.4(ii)]
.
Thus,
k_{0}=3
and
A\leqslant 2
. Then either
A=1
and
\lambda=2
, or
A=2
and
\lambda=5
since
\lambda\mid(Az-1,A+k_{0})
and
\lambda
is a prime number. In the former case,
k_{1}=3z+2\geqslant 5
, hence
k\geqslant 10
, but this contradicts by
[
17
, Theorem 1]
. Then
A=2
and
\lambda=5
and hence
k\leqslant 200
again by
[
17
, Theorem 1]
. On the other hand, either
k=75
or
150
since
k_{0}=3
and
25\mid k_{1}
. So
k_{1}=25
or
50
, and both are excluded since they contradict
k_{1}=4z+3
.

Suppose that
k_{0}=2
. Then
\lambda=A+2
by (
3.18
) since
\lambda\mid A+2
and
A\geqslant 1
. Then
2z+1=e\lambda
for some positive integer
e
since
\lambda\mid Az-1
. Therefore,

(3.19)

k_{1}=A(z+1)+1=(\lambda-2)\left(\frac{e\lambda-1}{2}+1\right)+1=\frac{1}{2}\lambda(e\lambda-2e+1)\text{.}

Further,
\lambda\mid 2e-1
since
\lambda^{2}\mid k_{1}
. Then
e=\frac{f\lambda+1}{2}
for some positive integer
f
, which substituted in (
3.19
), leads to

(3.20)

k=2k_{1}=\frac{1}{2}\lambda^{2}((\lambda-2)f+1)\text{.}

If
\lambda=2
then
k=2
, contrary to
\mathcal{D}
non-trivial. Thus
\lambda
is odd since
\lambda
is a prime number, and hence
f
is odd since
e=\frac{f\lambda+1}{2}
is an integer. As
k\leqslant 2\lambda^{2}(\lambda-1)
by
[
17
, Theorem 1]
, then
(\lambda-2)f+1\leqslant 4(\lambda-1)
. Hence,
f\leqslant 7
since
\lambda>2
. Actually, either
f=1
or
3
, or
f=5
and
\lambda=3
or
5
, or
f=7
and
\lambda=3
since both
f
and
\lambda
are odd.

Since

k_{1}=\left(k_{1},v_{1}\right)\cdot\left(k_{1},\frac{r}{\lambda}\right)\cdot\lambda=(z,A+1)\cdot(Az-1,A+2)\cdot\lambda

by Lemma
3.7
(1)–(2), (5) and
(Az-1,A+2)=\lambda
, it follows from (
3.20
) that

(z,A+1)=\left(\frac{f\lambda^{2}+\lambda-2}{4},\lambda-1\right)=\frac{1}{4}((\lambda-2)f+1)\text{.}

Thus
\frac{1}{4}((\lambda-2)f+1)\mid\lambda-1
, forcing
f=1
and
\lambda\equiv 1\pmod{4}
, or
f=\lambda=3
or
5
. The latter yields the tuples
\left(k_{0},v_{0},k_{1},v_{1},k,v,\lambda\right)=(2,16,9,16,18,256,3)
or
(2,66,100,196,200,12936,5)
. The former is ruled out by
[
18
, Lemma 3.5]
, whereas
A=3
in the latter. Then
A_{196}\unlhd G_{\Delta}^{\Delta}\leqslant S_{196}
by
[
65
, Theorem 1.2]
and
[
19
, Table B.4]
, but this contradicts
[
18
, Proposition 2.4(ii)]
. Thus
f=1
and
\lambda\equiv 1\pmod{4}
, and hence

\left(k_{0},v_{0},k_{1},v_{1}\right)=\left(2,\frac{1}{2}\left(\lambda^{2}+\lambda+2\right),\frac{1}{4}\lambda^{2}\left(\lambda-1\right),\frac{1}{2}\left(\lambda-1\right)\left(\lambda^{2}-2\right)\right)

with
\lambda\equiv 1\pmod{4}
, which is (1).

Assume that
\lambda
does not divide
v_{1}(v_{1}-1)
but divides
v_{0}(v_{0}-1)
. If
\lambda\mid v_{0}-1
then
\lambda\mid\frac{v_{0}-1}{k_{0}-1}
since
\lambda\geqslant k_{0}
, and hence
\lambda\mid v_{1}-1
since
v_{1}=A\left(zk_{0}+1\right)+1
and
\frac{v_{0}-1}{k_{0}-1}=zk_{0}+1
by Proposition
3.5
(2)(3), a contradiction. Therefore,
\lambda\mid v_{0}
. If
\lambda\mid\frac{v_{0}}{k_{0}}
, then
\lambda\mid k_{1}-1
since
k_{1}=A\frac{v_{0}}{k_{0}}+1
by Proposition
3.5
(3), whereas
\lambda\mid k_{1}
. Thus
\lambda\mid k_{0}
, and hence
\lambda=k_{0}
and
A=1
by Proposition
3.5
(4). Therefore
k_{1}=z\lambda-z+2
by Proposition
3.5
(3), and
hence
\lambda\mid z-2
. Then there is a positive integer
h
such that
z=h\lambda+2
. Then
k_{1}=\left(h\lambda+2\right)\left(\lambda-1\right)+2
, and hence
k=\lambda\left(h\lambda+2\right)\left(\lambda-1\right)+2\lambda
since
k_{0}=\lambda
. Then
h=1
since
k\leqslant 2\lambda^{2}(\lambda-1)
by
[
17
, Theorem 1]
. Therefore
k_{1}=\lambda\left(\lambda+1\right)
, and hence

\left(k_{0},v_{0},k_{1},v_{1}\right)=\left(\lambda,\lambda\left(\lambda^{2}+\lambda-1\right),\lambda\left(\lambda+1\right),\lambda^{2}+2\lambda+2\right)\text{,}

which is (2).

Finally, assume that
\lambda
divides neither
v_{1}(v_{1}-1)
nor
v_{0}(v_{0}-1)
. Hence,
\lambda
does not divide
v
since
v=v_{0}v_{1}
. Further,
\lambda>k_{0}
by Proposition
3.5
(1)(4). Assume that
\lambda\mid v-1
. Since
\lambda\mid k_{1}
and
k=k_{0}k_{1}
, it follows that
\lambda\mid k
. Then
\lambda\mid\frac{v-1}{k-1}
since
\lambda\nmid k-1
, and hence
\lambda\mid\frac{v_{0}-1}{k_{0}-1}
by (
2.1
) in Theorem
2.4
(1). So,
\lambda\mid v_{1}-1
since
v_{1}=A\left(zk_{0}+1\right)+1
and
\frac{v_{0}-1}{k_{0}-1}=zk_{0}+1
by Proposition
3.5
(2)(3), a contradiction. Thus
\lambda\nmid v(v-1)
, and we obtain (3).
∎

3.1.
Types of
\mathcal{D}_{1}

Based on Lemma
3.7
(5), we may define a
2
-design
\mathcal{D}_{1}
to be of
type I or II
according as
k_{1}=\left(k_{1},v_{1}\right)\cdot\left(k_{1},\frac{r}{\lambda}\right)\cdot\lambda
or
k_{1}=\left(k_{1},v_{1}\right)\cdot\left(k_{1},\frac{r}{\lambda}\right)
, respectively.
When
\mathcal{D}_{1}
is of type I, we say that
\mathcal{D}_{1}
is of
type Ia, Ib or Ic
according as (3), (2) or (1) of Proposition
3.8
holds, respectively.

When
\mathcal{D}_{1}
is of type II,
v_{0}
is even and it is shown in the proof of Lemma
3.7
(4) that
A=(k_{1}-1,v_{1}-1)=v_{0}-3
. Then
\lambda\geqslant v_{0}-2
by Proposition
3.5
(4) since
k_{0}=2
. If
\lambda=v_{0}-2
, then
v_{0}=4
since
\lambda
is a prime number. Then
v_{1}=4
and
k_{1}=3
, hence
\mathcal{D}_{1}
is a symmetric
1
-design with
k_{1}=v_{1}
, contrary to our assumptions. Also,
\lambda\neq v_{0}
, otherwise
\lambda\neq v_{0}=2
, contrary to Lemma
3.1
. Thus, either
\lambda=v_{0}-1
, or
\lambda>v_{0}
. When this occurs, we say that
\mathcal{D}_{1}
is of
type IIa or IIb
, respectively.

.

holds. Then
\mathcal{D}_{1}
is a

2
.

Moreover, if
\mathcal{D}_{1}
is of type Ia, it results that

(3.21)

A\left(z(k_{0}-1)+1\right)+1=(z,A+1)\cdot(Az-1,A+k_{0})\cdot\lambda

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{D}
be a flag-transitive point-imprimitive
2
-
(v,k,\lambda)
design with
\lambda
is a prime number. Then
\mathcal{D}_{1}
is a
2
-design by Theorem
2.4
since
\mathcal{D}_{1}
is not a symmetric
1
-design with
k{1}=v_{1}-1
by Hypothesis
3.4
. If
\mathcal{D}_{1}

v_{0},k_{0},\lambda_{0},v_{1},k_{1}

by Lemma

(4) according as

or

, respectively. Finally, if
\mathcal{D}_{1}

v_{0},k_{0},\lambda_{0},v_{1},k_{1}
and
