, and hence they are all ruled out. This completes the proof.
∎

Let
X
be any of the classical groups
SL_{n}(q)
,
Sp_{n}(q)
,
SU_{n}(q^{1/2})
or
\Omega^{\varepsilon}(q)
on
V=V_{n}(q)
, chosen to be minimal such that
G_{\Delta}^{\Sigma}\leqslant N_{\Gamma L_{n}(q)}(X)
. Firstly, we are going to prove that
G

Lemma 7.4
.

Assume that Hypothesis
7.2
holds. Let
\Delta\in\Sigma
, then the following hold:

(1)

\lambda
divides
\left|G_{\Delta}^{\Sigma}\cap SL_{n}(q)\right|
, and
\lambda
does not divide
\left|G_{\Delta}^{\Sigma}:G_{\Delta}^{\Sigma}\cap SL_{n}(q)\right|
;

(2)

If
G^{\Sigma}
acts point-
2
-transitively on
\mathcal{D}_{1}
, then one of the following holds:

(a)

SL_{n}(q)\trianglelefteq G_{\Delta}^{\Sigma}\leqslant\Gamma L_{n}(q)
,
n\geqslant 4
;

(b)

Sp_{n}(q)\trianglelefteq G_{\Delta}^{\Sigma}\leqslant\Gamma Sp_{n}(q)
,
n\geqslant 10
.

Proof.

Assume that
\lambda
does not divide
\left|G_{\Delta}^{\Sigma}\cap SL_{n}(q)\right|
. Then
\lambda
divides
\left|G_{\Delta}^{\Sigma}:G_{\Delta}^{\Sigma}\cap SL_{n}(q)\right|
since
\lambda
divides
\left|G^{\Sigma}\right|
, and hence
\lambda
divides
\left|\Gamma L_{n}(q):SL_{n}(q)\right|
. Thus
\lambda\mid(q-1)\cdot\log_{p}(q)
, and hence
\lambda\mid\log_{p}(q)
since
v_{1}=q^{n}=p^{d}
and
\lambda\nmid v_{1}-1
by Theorem
3.9
since
\mathcal{D}_{1}
if of type Ia. Then
p^{d}\leqslant 2\lambda^{2}(\lambda-1)
by
[
17
, Theorem 1]
, and hence
p=2
and
\lambda=d=n=5,7
or
11
arguing as in
the proof of Lemma
7.3
.
Therefore,
G^{\Sigma}\leqslant SL_{n}(2)
with
n=5,7
or
11
, but
\lambda
does not divide
\left|G^{\Sigma}:G^{\Sigma}\cap SL_{n}(q)\right|
. This proves (1).

Suppose that
n\leqslant 3
. Then
n=3
and
\lambda=\frac{q+1}{e}
for some integer
e\geqslant 1

by (1) and since
\lambda\nmid v_{1}(v_{1}-1)
. Actually,
e=1
and
\lambda=q+1
since
v_{1}\leqslant 2\lambda^{2}(\lambda-1)
. Therefore,
q=2^{2^{j}}
with
j\geqslant 1
since
\lambda
is a prime and
\lambda>3
.
Since
A\frac{v_{0}-1}{k_{0}-1}=q^{3}-1
, and
k_{1}=A\frac{v_{0}}{k_{0}}+1
, it follows that
k=Av_{0}+k_{0}
and hence

k=k_{0}k_{1}=\left(q^{3}-1\right)(k_{0}-1)+A+k_{0}>q^{3}(k_{0}-1)\text{.}

Hence
2\leqslant k_{0}\leqslant 4
or
(k_{0},q)=(5,4)
since
k\leqslant 2\lambda^{2}(\lambda-1)=2q(q+1)^{2}
and
k_{0}\geqslant 2
. The latter is ruled out since
k_{0}<\lambda
. Further, since
\lambda=q+1
divides
k_{1}
, and hence
k
, it results
\lambda\mid-2k_{0}+2+A
and hence
\lambda<A
, contrary to Proposition
3.5
(4). Thus, we obtain
n\geqslant 4
.

Assume that
G^{\Sigma}
acts point-
2
-transitively on
\mathcal{D}_{1}
.
Then either
SL_{n}(q)\trianglelefteq G_{\Delta}^{\Sigma}
, or
Sp_{n}(q)\trianglelefteq G_{\Delta}^{\Sigma}
(
n
is even), or
G_{\Delta}^{\Sigma}\cong A_{7}
,
v_{1}=2^{4}
and
\lambda=7
by
[
32
, (B)]
since
n>1
,
\lambda
divides
\left|G_{\Delta}^{\Sigma}\cap SL_{n}(q)\right|
,
\lambda\nmid v_{1}(v_{1}-1)
and
\lambda>3
. Assume that the latter occurs. Then
v_{1}-1=A\frac{v_{0}-1}{k_{0}-1}
and
\frac{v_{0}-1}{k_{0}-1}>\lambda=7
force
A=1
. Then
zk_{0}+1=\frac{v_{0}-1}{k_{0}-1}=15
by Proposition
3.5
(2). So
(z,k_{0})=(2,7)

or
(7,2)
since
\lambda\geqslant k_{0}\geqslant 2
. Then
\left(v_{0},k_{0},v_{1},k_{1}\right)=(91,7,16,14)
or
(16,2,16,9)

since
k_{1}=A\frac{v_{0}}{k_{0}}+1
. The former is ruled out by Theorem
3.9
since it contradicts
\lambda\nmid v_{0}
, the latter since
9\mid r_{1}
but
r_{1}\mid 15\cdot 8\cdot 7
. Thus, either
SL_{n}(q)\trianglelefteq G_{\Delta}^{\Sigma}
,
n\geqslant 4
, and we obtain (2.a), or
Sp_{n}(q)\trianglelefteq G_{\Delta}^{\Sigma}
(
n
is even),
n\geqslant 4
. In the latter case, either
n=6
and
\lambda=q^{2}+1
, or
n=8
,
\lambda\mid q^{2}+\varepsilon q+1
,
\varepsilon=\pm
, since
\lambda\mid\left|G_{\Delta}^{\Sigma}\cap SL_{n}(q)\right|
by Lemma
7.4
(1) and
\lambda\nmid v_{1}(v_{1}-1)
. On the other hand,

(7.2)

4q^{n}\leqslant v_{0}v_{1}\leqslant 2\lambda^{2}(\lambda-1)

by Lemma
3.1
and
[
17
, Theorem 1]
. Thus, both cases are excluded since they do not fulfill (
7.2
). Thus
n\geqslant 10
, and we obtain (2.b).
∎

Lemma 7.5
.

Assume that Hypothesis
7.2
holds. If
SL_{n}(q)\trianglelefteq G_{\Delta}^{\Sigma}\leqslant\Gamma L_{n}(q)
,
n\geqslant 4
, then
G_{(\Sigma)}=1
.

Proof.

Assume that
G_{(\Sigma)}\neq 1
. Then
r_{1}=\frac{v_{0}-1}{k_{0}-1}\lambda
and
b_{1}=\frac{v_{1}(v_{0}-1)\lambda}{k_{1}(k_{0}-1)}
by Lemma
6.1
(2). Moreover,
\lambda\mid\frac{q^{n-i}-1}{q-1}
with
i\geqslant 1
since
\lambda\nmid v_{1}(v_{1}-1)
, and so
r_{1}=\frac{q^{n}-1}{A}\lambda
.

We are going to prove the assertion in two steps.

Claim 1
.

\lambda=\frac{q^{n-1}-1}{q-1}
and
r_{1}=\frac{q^{n}-1}{A}\cdot\frac{q^{n-1}-1}{q-1}
.

Let
K
be the stabilizer in
G^{\Sigma}
of a block
B^{\Sigma}
of
\mathcal{D}_{1}
containing
\Delta\in\Sigma
. Then
K_{\Delta}
contains a Sylow
p
-subgroup
of
G_{\Delta}^{\Sigma}
since
r_{1}
is coprime to
p
, and hence
K_{\Delta}\leqslant G_{\Delta}^{\Sigma}
with
[q^{n-1}]:GL_{n-1}(q)\trianglelefteq G_{\Delta}^{\Sigma}\leqslant[q^{n-1}]:\Gamma L_{n-1}(q)
by
[
34
, Proposition 4.1.17(II)]
. Thus,

(7.3)

{n-1\brack j}_{q}\mid\frac{q^{n}-1}{A}\frac{q^{n-i}-1}{q-1}

for some
1\leqslant j\leqslant n/2
. Then
q^{j(n-j)}\leqslant{n-1\brack j}_{q}<q^{2n-i}
, and hence
n(j-2)<j^{2}-i\leqslant j^{2}-1
since
i\geqslant 1
. If
j>2
then
n<j+2+\frac{4-i}{j-2}\leqslant n/2+5
, and hence either
j=3
,
n=6,7
and
i=1,2
. However, it is easy to see that none of these remaining cases fulfills (
7.3
), and hence they are all excluded. Thus,
j=1
or
2
.

Assume that
j=2
. Then (
7.3
) implies

\frac{\left(q^{n-1}-1\right)\left(q^{n-2}-1\right)}{\left(q-1\right)\left(q^{2}-1\right)}\mid\left(q^{n}-1\right)\frac{q^{n-i}-1}{q-1}

and hence
i=1
and
q^{n-2}-1\mid\left(q^{n}-1\right)\left(q^{2}-1\right)
. Thus, we obtain
n=4
,
i=1
,
\lambda=q^{2}+q+1
and
r_{1}=\frac{q^{4}-1}{A}(q^{2}+q+1)
, which is the assertion for
n=4
.

Assume that
j=1
. Hence (
7.3
) implies
i=1
.
Moreover
\frac{q^{n-1}-1}{q-1}\mid\lambda
since
\frac{q^{n-1}-1}{q-1}

divides
\frac{q^{n}-1}{A}\lambda
, and hence
\lambda=\frac{q^{n-1}-1}{q-1}
since
\lambda\mid\left|G_{\Delta}^{\Sigma}\cap GL_{n}(q)\right|
and
\lambda\nmid q^{n}(q^{n}-1)
. Therefore,
r_{1}=\frac{q^{n}-1}{A}\cdot\frac{q^{n-1}-1}{q-1}
.

Claim 2
.

G_{(\Sigma)}=1
.

Then the group
K/T_{B^{\Sigma}}
is isomorphic to a subgroup
J
of
G_{\Delta}^{\Sigma}

containing
K_{\Delta}
and such that
\left|G_{\Delta}^{\Sigma}:J\right|=\frac{r_{1}\left|T_{B^{\Sigma}}\right|}{k_{1}}
. Then
\left|SL_{n}(q):J\cap SL_{n}(q)\right|=\left|SL_{n}(q)J:J\right|
divides
\frac{r_{1}\left|T_{B^{\Sigma}}\right|}{k_{1}}
, and hence

(7.4)

\left|SL_{n}(q):J\cap SL_{n}(q)\right|=\frac{r_{1}\left|T_{B^{\Sigma}}\right|}{k_{1}}\text{.}

On the other hand,
k_{1}=\alpha\frac{q^{n-1}-1}{q-1}
with
\alpha\geqslant 1

since
\lambda\mid k_{1}
. Moreover

(7.5)

\frac{q(q-1)}{2}<\frac{q^{n}(q-1)}{2(q^{n-1}-1)}\leqslant\alpha

since
k_{1}\geqslant\frac{v_{1}}{2}
, and so
k_{1}>\frac{q\left(q^{n-1}-1\right)}{2}
. Then (
7.4
) implies

\left|SL_{n}(q):J\cap SL_{n}(q)\right|=\frac{r_{1}\left|T_{B^{\Sigma}}\right|}{k_{1}}<2\frac{(q^{n}-1)\left|T_{B^{\Sigma}}\right|}{Aq(q-1)}\leqslant 2\frac{(q^{n}-1)(z,A+1)}{Aq(q-1)}

since
\left|T_{B^{\Sigma}}\right|\mid\left(k_{1},v_{1}\right)
and
\left(k_{1},v_{1}\right)\mid(z,A+1)
by Lemma
3.7
(2).

If
Aq<2(z,A+1)
then either
q=3
and
A=1
, or
q=2
and
A+1\mid z
. The former is ruled out since
3
divides the order of
T_{B^{\Sigma}}
and hence
A+1
, which is not the case. Thus
q=2
and hence
\lambda=2^{n-1}-1
and
k_{1}=2^{n}-2
since
\alpha>1
by (
7.5
) and
k_{1}<v_{1}
. Then
\frac{v_{0}-1}{k_{0}-1}=2^{n}-1
and
\frac{v_{0}}{k_{0}}=2^{n}-3
, and hence
k_{0}=2^{n-1}-1=\lambda
, which is contrary to Theorem
3.9
.

If
Aq\geqslant 2(z,A+1)
then
\left|SL_{n}(q):J\cap SL_{n}(q)\right|<\frac{q^{n}-1}{q-1}
, and hence either
SL_{n}(q)\leqslant J
or
n=4
and
q=2
by
[
34
, Proposition
5.2.1(i) and Theorem 5.2.2]
since
n\geqslant 4
. In the latter case,
k_{1}<16
and
\lambda=7
divides
k_{1}
. Thus
k_{1}=\lambda=7
, and hence
A=3
and
k_{0}=2
by Proposition
3.5
(4) since
k_{0}\geqslant 2
. Therefore,
\frac{v_{0}-1}{k_{0}-1}=5
by Proposition
3.5
(3), contrary to
\frac{v_{0}-1}{k_{0}-1}>\lambda=7
. Therefore,
SL_{n}(q)\leqslant J
and hence
SL_{n}(q)\leqslant K/T_{B^{\Sigma}}
.

If
(q,n)=(2,6)
, then
\lambda=31
and hence
k_{1}=62
since
\lambda\mid k_{1}
and
v_{1}/2\leqslant k_{1}<v_{1}
. Then
\frac{v_{0}-1}{k_{0}-1}=63
and
A=1
since
v_{1}=A\frac{v_{0}-1}{k_{0}-1}+1
and
\frac{v_{0}}{k_{0}}=61
since
v_{1}=A\frac{v_{0}-1}{k_{0}-1}+1
. So,
k_{0}=v_{0}=31
, contrary to to Theorem
3.9
. Thus
(q,n)=(2,6)
, and hence
K
contains an element
\vartheta
of order a primitive prime divisor of
q^{n}-1
by Zsigmondy Theorem
[
34
, Theorem 5.2.14]
. Moreover,
\vartheta
normalizes
T_{B^{\Sigma}}
, and hence either
T_{B^{\Sigma}}=T
or
T_{B^{\Sigma}}=1
by
[
25
, Theorem 3.5]
. The latter implies
T:SL_{n}(q)
and hence
k_{1}=v_{1}
, which is not the case. Thus
T_{B^{\Sigma}}=1
and
SL_{n}(q)\leqslant J
, and hence
K
contains a normal subgroup
Q

isomorphic to
SL_{n}(q)
. Then
Q
fixes a unique point
\Delta^{\prime}

of
\mathcal{D}_{1}
by
[
33
, Theorem 2.14(ii)]
, and hence
Q\trianglelefteq K\leqslant G_{\Delta^{\prime}}^{\Sigma}
. Then
k_{1}=v_{1}-1=q^{n}-1
since
Q
transitively on the point set of
\mathcal{D}_{1}
not containing
\left\{\Delta^{\prime}\right\}
and
1<k_{1}<v_{1}
. However, this is
impossible since
\lambda=\frac{q^{n-1}-1}{q-1}
divides
k_{1}
and
n\geqslant 4
. This completes the proof.
∎

Lemma 7.6
.

Assume that Hypothesis
7.2
holds. If
Sp_{n}(q)\trianglelefteq G_{\Delta}^{\Sigma}\leqslant\Gamma Sp_{n}(q)
with
n\geqslant 10
, then
G_{(\Sigma)}=1
.

Proof.

Assume that
G_{(\Sigma)}\neq 1
. Then
r_{1}=\frac{v_{0}-1}{k_{0}-1}\lambda
and
b_{1}=\frac{v_{1}(v_{0}-1)\lambda}{k_{1}(k_{0}-1)}
by Lemma
6.1
(2). Moreover,
\lambda\mid q^{n/2-i}+\varepsilon 1
, with
1\leqslant i<n
and
\varepsilon=\pm
by Lemma
7.4
(1) since
\lambda\nmid v_{1}(v_{1}-1)
, and so
r_{1}=\frac{q^{n}-1}{A}\lambda
is coprime to
p
.

Let
K
be the stabilizer in
G^{\Sigma}
of a block
B^{\Sigma}
of
\mathcal{D}_{1}
containing
\Delta
for some
\Delta\in\Sigma
. The
group
K_{\Delta}
is a subgroup of
G_{\Delta}^{\Sigma}
containing a
Sylow
p
-subgroup of
G_{\Delta}^{\Sigma}
since
r_{1}
is coprime to
p
. If
K_{\Delta}\cap Sp_{n}(q)<Sp_{n}(q)
, then
K_{\Delta}\cap Sp_{n}(q)

lies in maximal parabolic subgroup of
Sp_{n}(q)
. Hence,

(7.6)

\prod_{j=0}^{h-1}\frac{q^{n-2j}-1}{q^{j+1}-1}\mid\frac{q^{n}-1}{A}\lambda\text{,}

by
[
34
, Proposition 4.1.19(II)]
. Assume that
h>2
. Since
n\geqslant 10
and
h\leqslant n/2
, it follows that
n-4>h
. For each
s=0,2,4
, the integer
\prod_{j=1}^{h-1}\frac{q^{n-2j}-1}{q^{j+1}-1}
is divisible by a primitive prime divisor of
q^{n-s}-1
, whereas
\frac{q^{n}-1}{A}\left(q^{n/2-i}+\varepsilon 1\right)
is not. If
h=2
then
\frac{q^{n}-1}{q-1}\cdot\frac{q^{n-2}-1}{q^{2}-1}\mid\frac{q^{n}-1}{A}\left(q^{n/2-i}+\varepsilon 1\right)
, forcing
i=1
since
\frac{q^{n}-1}{q-1}\cdot\frac{q^{n-2}-1}{q^{2}-1}
is divisible by a primitive prime
divisor of
q^{n-s}-1
for each
s=0,2
. Then
A\left(q^{n/2-1}-\varepsilon 1\right)\mid(q-1)(q^{2}-1)
, and hence
q^{n/2-1}\leqslant q^{3}-q-q^{2}+2<q^{3}
and hence
n/2-1<3
, which implies
n\leqslant 8
, a contradiction. Thus
h=1
, and hence
A\mid(q-1)\lambda
by (
7.6
). Actually,
A\mid q-1
since
A<\lambda
by Proposition
3.5
(4). and hence
K_{\Delta}\leqslant[q^{n-1}]:\left((q-1)\times Sp_{n-2}(q)\right).(2,q-1)\cdot\log_{p}q
by
[
34
, Proposition 4.1.19(II)]
. Recall that
K_{\Delta}
is a
subgroup of
G_{\Delta}^{\Sigma}
containing a Sylow
p
-subgroup of
G_{\Delta}^{\Sigma}
, and let
R=\frac{K_{\Delta}}{[q^{n-1}]}
. If
R\cap Sp_{n-2}(q)<Sp_{n-2}(q)
, then
R\cap Sp_{n-2}(q)
lies in a maximal parabolic subgroup of
Sp_{n-2}(q)
, and hence

\frac{q^{n}-1}{q-1}\cdot\prod_{j=0}^{m-1}\frac{q^{n-2-2j}-1}{q^{j+1}-1}\mid\frac{q^{n}-1}{A}\left(q^{n/2-i}+\varepsilon 1\right)

Since
n\geqslant 10
and
m\leqslant n/2
then
n-2>m
and so
\prod_{j=0}^{m-1}\frac{q^{n-2-2j}-1}{q^{j+1}-1}
is divisible by a primitive prime divisor of
q^{n-s}-1
with
s=0,2,4
for
m>1
, whereas
\frac{q^{n}-1}{A}\left(q^{n/2-i}+\varepsilon 1\right)
is not. Thus,
m=1
and
\frac{q^{n}-1}{q-1}\cdot\frac{q^{n-2}-1}{q-1}\mid\frac{q^{n}-1}{A}\left(q^{n/2-i}+\varepsilon 1\right)
. Now, arguing as above, we obtain
A\left(q^{n/2-1}-\varepsilon 1\right)\mid(q-1)(q^{2}-1)
and we reach a
contradiction since
n\geqslant 10
. Thus
Sp_{n-2}(q)\leqslant R
, and hence
K
contains proper subgroup of order
K_{\Delta}
such that
Sp_{n-2}(q)\leqslant\frac{K_{\Delta}}{[q^{n-1}]}
. Then either
K
lies in a maximal parabolic subgroup of
G^{\Sigma}
of type
P_{1}
, or
Sp_{n-2}(q)\trianglelefteq K
since
K
contains a Sylow
p
-subgroup of
G_{\Delta}^{\Sigma}
. In the former
case,
k_{1}\mid(q-1)(2,q-1)\cdot\log_{p}q
since
K\leqslant[q^{n-1}]:\left((q-1)\times Sp_{n-2}(q)\right).(2,q-1)\cdot\log_{p}q
.
Then
\lambda\mid(q-1)(2,q-1)\cdot\log_{p}q
since
\lambda\mid k_{1}
,
and hence
\lambda\mid\log_{p}q
since
\lambda\nmid v_{1}-1
. Now, the same argument used in the proof of Lemma
7.4
(1) rules out this case. Thus
Sp_{n}(q)\trianglelefteq K
, and hence
b_{1}\mid q^{n}(q-1)(2,q-1)\log_{p}q
. Therefore
q^{n}\cdot\frac{k_{1}}{\lambda}\cdot\frac{v_{0}-1}{k_{0}-1}\mid q^{n}\cdot(q-1)\cdot(2,q-1)\cdot\log_{p}q
and hence

\frac{q^{n}}{2}\leqslant k_{1}\leqslant(q-1)\cdot(2,q-1)\cdot\log_{p}\leqslant 2q^{1/2}(q-1)

since
\frac{v_{0}-1}{k_{0}-1}\geqslant\lambda
, which does not admit admissible solutions. This completes the proof.
∎

Theorem 7.7
.

Assume that Hypothesis
7.2
holds. Then
G^{\Sigma}
does not act point-
2
-transitively on
\mathcal{D}_{1}
.

Proof.

Assume that
G^{\Sigma}
acts point-
2
-transitively on
\mathcal{D}_{1}
. Then
G_{(\Sigma)}=1
, and either
SL_{n}(q)\trianglelefteq G_{\Delta}\leqslant\Gamma L_{n}(q)
with
n\geqslant 4
, or
Sp_{n}(q)\trianglelefteq G_{\Delta}\leqslant\Gamma Sp_{n}(q)
with
n\geqslant 10
by Lemmas
7.4
(2),
7.5
and
7.6
.
Note that,
\lambda\nmid v(v_{1}-1)
by Theorem
3.9
since
\mathcal{D}_{1}
is of type Ia. Hence,
\lambda\mid\left|G_{\Delta}^{\Delta}\right|
by Lemma
4.5
.

The group
Soc(G_{\Delta}^{\Delta})
is either an
elementary abelian
p
-group or a non-abelian simple group by the point-
2
-transitivity of
G_{\Delta}^{\Delta}
on
\mathcal{D}_{1}
for
k_{0}=2
, by
[
8
, Main Theorem]
for
k_{0}\geqslant 3
and
\mu=\lambda
, and by
[
63
, Theorem 1]
for
k_{0}\geqslant 3
and
\mu=1
.

Assume that
SL_{n}(q)\trianglelefteq G_{\Delta}\leqslant\Gamma L_{n}(q)
with
n\geqslant 4
. If
SL_{n}(q)\trianglelefteq G_{(\Delta)}
then
\lambda\mid\left|G_{\Delta}:SL_{n}(q)\right|
since
\lambda\mid\left|G_{\Delta}^{\Delta}\right|
, but this contradicts Lemma
7.4
(1). Thus,
G_{(\Delta)}
is the center of
SL_{n}(q)
and
PSL_{n}(q)\trianglelefteq G_{\Delta}^{\Delta}\leqslant\Gamma L_{n}(q)
with
n\geqslant 4
by the previous argument on the strcuture of
Soc(G_{\Delta}^{\Delta})
. Similarly, we have
Sp_{n}(q)\nleq G_{(\Delta)}
when
Sp_{n}(q)\trianglelefteq G_{\Delta}\leqslant\Gamma Sp_{n}(q)
with
n\geqslant 10
, and hence
PSp_{n}(q)\trianglelefteq G_{\Delta}^{\Delta}\leqslant P\Gamma Sp_{n}(q)
with
n\geqslant 10
. Moreover, in both cases, one has
\lambda\leqslant\frac{q^{n-1}-1}{q-1}
since
\lambda\nmid v_{1}(v_{1}-1)
, being
\mathcal{D}_{1}
is of type Ia, and hence

v_{0}=\frac{q^{n}-1}{A}(k_{0}-1)+1\leqslant\left(q^{n}-1\right)\lambda\leqslant\left(q^{n}-1\right)\frac{q^{n-1}-1}{q-1}<q\frac{q^{n}-1}{q-1}\cdot\frac{q^{n-1}-1}{q-1}\text{.}

Then Proposition
6.2
implies the exclusion of
PSp_{n}(q)\trianglelefteq G_{\Delta}^{\Delta}\leqslant P\Gamma Sp_{n}(q)
with
n\geqslant 10
, and
n
odd,
v_{0}=\frac{q^{n}-1}{q-1}
and
k_{0}=q^{2}+q+1
for
PSL_{n}(q)\trianglelefteq G_{\Delta}^{\Delta}\leqslant P\Gamma L_{n}(q)
. Thus
\frac{v_{0}-1}{k_{0}-1}=\frac{q^{n-1}-1}{q+1}
since
n
is odd, and hence
q^{n-1}-1\mid\frac{q^{n}-1}{A}\cdot\left(q+1\right)
since
v_{1}-1=A\frac{v_{0}-1}{k_{0}-1}
and
v_{1}=q^{n}
. So
q^{n-1}-1\mid q^{2}-1
, contrary to
n\geqslant 4
.
∎

Lemma 7.8
.

Assume that Hypothesis
7.2
holds. Then
G_{\Delta}^{\Sigma}
does not contain
X
in its natural
action on
V_{n}(q)
.

Proof.

Assume that
G_{\Delta}^{\Sigma}
contains
X
in its natural action on
V=V_{n}(q)
. If
X\cong SL_{n}(q)
or
Sp_{n}(q)
then
G^{\Sigma}
acts
point-
2
-transitively on
\mathcal{D}_{1}
, which is not the case by
Theorem
7.7
. Hence, either
X\cong SU_{n}(q^{1/2})
or
X\cong\Omega^{\varepsilon}(q)
with
\varepsilon\in\left\{\pm,\circ\right\}
. Moreover,
\lambda\mid\left|X\right|
by Lemma
7.4
(1), and
\lambda\nmid v_{1}(v_{1}-1)
by Theorem
3.9
since
\mathcal{D}_{1}
is of type Ia.

Suppose that
X\cong SU_{n}(q^{1/2})
. Then
\frac{r_{1}}{(r_{1},\lambda_{1})}
divides the number of nonzero isotropic vectors of
V
, hence

\frac{r_{1}}{(r_{1},\lambda_{1})}\mid(q^{n}-1,((q^{1/2})^{n}-(-1)^{n})((q^{1/2})^{n-1}-(-1)^{n-1}))

If
n
is even, then
\frac{r_{1}}{(r_{1},\lambda_{1})}=\frac{v_{0}-1}{k_{0}-1}=2(q^{n/2}-1)
with
q
odd since
\frac{r_{1}}{(r_{1},\lambda_{1})}>q^{n/2}
by Theorem
7.1
(4). Then
A=\frac{q^{n/2}+1}{2}
by
Proposition
3.5
(3) since
v_{1}=q^{n}
. Therefore,
\lambda\geqslant\frac{q^{n/2}+1}{2}+1
by Proposition
3.5
(4). On the other hand,
\lambda\mid q^{i/2}-(-1)^{i}
with
i<n
since
\lambda\mid\left|X\right|
and
\lambda\nmid v_{1}(v_{1}-1)
. So
\frac{q^{n/2}+1}{2}+1\leqslant\lambda\leqslant q^{(n-1)/2}-1
with
q
odd, which is a
contradiction.

If
n
is odd, then
\frac{r_{1}}{(r_{1},\lambda_{1})}=\frac{(q^{n/2}+1)(q-1)}{\theta}
for some
\theta\geqslant 1
. Hence
A=\frac{\left(q^{n/2}-1\right)\theta}{q^{1/2}-1}
and
\lambda\geqslant A+1=\frac{\left(q^{n/2}-1\right)\theta}{q^{1/2}-1}+1>\frac{q^{n/2}-1}{q^{1/2}-1}
.
However,
\lambda\mid\frac{q^{i/2}-(-1)^{i}}{q^{1/2}-(-1)^{i}}
with
i<n

since
\lambda\mid\left|X\right|
,
\lambda\nmid v_{1}(v_{1}-1)

and
\lambda
is a prime. So
\frac{q^{n/2}-1}{q^{1/2}-1}<\frac{q^{i/2}-(-1)^{i}}{q^{1/2}-(-1)^{i}}\leqslant\frac{q^{(n-1)/2}+1}{q^{1/2}-1}
,
which is impossible for
n\geqslant 3
.

Suppose that
X\cong\Omega^{\varepsilon}(q)
,
\varepsilon\in\left\{\pm,\circ\right\}
. If
\varepsilon=\circ
then
\frac{r_{1}}{(r_{1},\lambda_{1})}\mid(q^{n}-1,q^{n-1}-1)=q-1
since
q^{n-1}-1
is the
number of nonzero singular vectors of
V
, but this is contrary to
\frac{r_{1}}{(r_{1},\lambda_{1})}>q^{n/2}
. Thus,
n
is even. If
\varepsilon=+
, then
\frac{r_{1}}{(r_{1},\lambda_{1})}\mid(q^{n}-1,(q^{n/2}-1)(q^{n/2-1}+1))
since
(q^{n/2}-1)(q^{n/2-1}+1)
is the number of nonzero singular vectors of
V
. So,
q
is odd,
\frac{r_{1}}{(r_{1},\lambda_{1})}=2(q^{n/2}-1)
,
A=\frac{q^{n/2}+1}{2}
and
\lambda\geqslant\frac{q^{n/2}+1}{2}+1
, whereas
\lambda\mid q^{i/2}-1
since
i<n
,

\lambda\mid\left|X\right|
and
\lambda\nmid v_{1}(v_{1}-1)
,
and we reach a contradiction. Therefore,
\varepsilon=-
. Therefore
\frac{r_{1}}{(r_{1},\lambda_{1})}\mid(q^{n}-1,(q^{n/2}+1)(q^{n/2-1}-1))
since
(q^{n/2}+1)(q^{n/2-1}-1)
is the number of nonzero singular vectors of
V
,
and hence
\frac{r_{1}}{(r_{1},\lambda_{1})}=\frac{(q^{n/2}+1)(q-1)}{\theta}
for some
\theta\geqslant 1
. Then
A=\frac{\left(q^{n/2}-1\right)\theta}{q-1}
and
\lambda>\frac{q^{n/2}-1}{q-1}+1
. On the other hand,
\lambda\mid\frac{q^{i}-1}{q-1}
with
i<2n
since
\lambda\mid\left|X\right|
and
\lambda\nmid v_{1}(v_{1}-1)
and
\lambda\nmid v_{1}(v_{1}-1)
, and again we reach a contradiction. This
completes the proof.
∎

7.1.
Aschbacher’s theorem.

Recall that
G_{\Delta}^{\Sigma}\leqslant N_{\Gamma L_{n}(q)}(X)
and
X
is any of the classical groups
SL_{n}(q)
,
Sp_{n}(q)
,
SU_{n}(q^{1/2})
or
\Omega^{\varepsilon}(q)
. The case where where
X\leqslant G_{0}
has been settled in Lemma
7.8
, hence, in the sequel, we assume that
G_{\Delta}^{\Sigma}
does not contain
X
. Now, according to
[
4
]
, one of the following holds:

(I)

G_{\Delta}^{\Sigma}
lies in a maximal member of one of the geometric classes
\mathcal{C}_{i}
of
N_{\Gamma L_{n}(q)}(X)
,
i=1,...,7
(the case
\mathcal{C}_{8}
is excluded in Lemma
7.8
);

(II)

\left(G_{\Delta}^{\Sigma}\right)^{(\infty)}
is a quasisimple group, and its action on
V_{n}(q)
is absolutely irreducible and not realizable over any proper subfield of
\mathbb{F}_{q}
.

Description of each class
\mathcal{C}_{i}
,
i=1,...,8
, can be found in
[
34
, Chapter 4]
.

The proof strategy used by Liebeck in
[
37
]
to classify the
2
-
(q^{n},k^{\prime},1)
designs
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
(linear spaces) admitting flag transitive group
H=T:H_{0}\leqslant A\Gamma L_{n}(q)
, with
T
,
V
,
n
,
q
having the same meaning as ours, is group-theoretical and is primarily to filter out the possible candidates for the group
H_{0}
with respect to the properties

(7.7)

r^{\prime}\mid(q^{n}-1,c_{1},...,c_{j},\left|H_{0}\right|)\text{~and~}r^{\prime}>q^{n/2}\textit{,}

where
r^{\prime}=\frac{q^{n}-1}{k^{\prime}-1}
, and
c_{1},...,c_{j}
are the lengths of the
H_{0}
-orbit on the set of nonzero vectors of
V
, by
[
37
, Lemma 2.1]
. In other words, in Liebeck’s paper the admissible groups are those affording a (semi)linear representation with non-trivial orbits divisible by the same factor of size greater than square root of the size of the vector space. In our context, the constraints in (
7.7
) are replaced by

(7.8)

\frac{r_{1}}{(r_{1},\lambda_{1})}\mid(q^{n}-1,c_{1},...,c_{j},\left|H_{0}\right|)\text{~and~}\frac{r_{1}}{(r_{1},\lambda_{1})}>\frac{q^{n/2}}{\sqrt{2}}\text{,}

which hold by Theorem
5.1
(3)–(4). Hence, we may use Liebeck’s argument with
G^{\Sigma}
and
\frac{r_{1}}{(r_{1},\lambda_{1})}
in the role of
H
and
r^{\prime}
to reduce our investigation to the cases where (
7.8
) is fulfilled. We provide in one exemplary case (namely, when
G_{\Delta}^{\Sigma}
lies in a maximal
\mathcal{C}_{2}
subgroup of
N_{\Gamma L_{n}(q)}(X)
) more guidance and proof details to help the reader control the transfer from the linear space case investigated by Liebeck to our case. For the remaining groups in (I) or (II), the proof strategy, which relies on Liebeck’s argument, is similar. The corresponding admissible cases are contained in Tables
7
,
8
,
9
and
10
, respectively. Hence, the objective of this section is to prove the following result by using the above mentioned adaptation of Liebeck’s argument.

Proposition 7.9
.

Assume that Hypothesis
3.12
holds. Then
\left(G_{\Delta}^{\Sigma}\right)^{(\infty)}
is a quasisimple group, and its action on
V_{n}(q)
is absolutely irreducible and not realizable over any proper subfield of
\mathbb{F}_{q}
.

Proof.

Assume that Hypothesis
3.12
holds. Then the conclusions of Theorem
5.1
hold. If
G^{\Sigma}
is of affine type then Hypothesis
7.2
holds, and hence
G^{\Sigma}
is as in (I) or (II) above. Assume that
G_{\Delta}^{\Sigma}
lies in a maximal member of one of the geometric classes
\mathcal{C}_{i}
of
N_{\Gamma L_{n}(q)}(X)
,
i=1,...,7
. The group
G_{\Delta}^{\Sigma}
does not lie maximal member of type
\mathcal{C}_{1}
since
G_{\Delta}^{\Sigma}
acts irreducibly on
V_{n}(q)
. Moreover, by the definition of
q
,
G_{\Delta}^{\Sigma}
does not lie in a
member of
\mathcal{C}_{3}
. If
G_{\Delta}^{\Sigma}
lies in a maximal member of one of the geometric classes
\mathcal{C}_{2}
of
N_{\Gamma L_{n}(q)}(X)
. The group
G_{\Delta}^{\Sigma}
does not lie maximal member of type
\mathcal{C}_{1}
since
G_{\Delta}^{\Sigma}
acts irreducibly on
V_{n}(q)
. Moreover, by the definition of
q
,
G_{\Delta}^{\Sigma}
does not lie in a
member of
\mathcal{C}_{3}
of
N_{\Gamma L_{n}(q)}(X)
. Hence, assume that
G_{\Delta}^{\Sigma}
lies in a maximal
\mathcal{C}_{2}
-subgroup of of
N_{\Gamma L_{n}(q)}(X)
. Then
G_{\Delta}^{\Sigma}
preserves a sum decomposition
V=V_{1}\oplus\cdots\oplus V_{n/a}
,
n/a\geqslant 2
, with
V_{i}\cong V_{a}(q)
for
i=1,...,n/a
. Hence,
G_{\Delta}^{\Sigma}\leqslant N
, where
N\cong\Gamma L_{a}(q)\wr S_{t}
(see
[
34
, Section 4.2]
). Now,
N=(L_{1}\times\cdots\times L_{n/a}):K
with
L_{i}\cong\Gamma L_{a}(q)
acting transitively on
V_{i}^{\ast}
for each
i=1,...,n/a
and
K\cong S_{t}
acting transitively on
\{V_{1},...,V_{n/a}\}
. Thus
\bigcup_{i=1}^{n/a}V^{\ast}_{i}
is a
N
-orbit, and hence it is a union of
G_{\Delta}^{\Sigma}
-orbits. Then
\frac{r_{1}}{(r_{1},\lambda_{1})}\mid\frac{n}{a}(q^{a}-1)
by Theorem
5.1
(5) since the size of
\bigcup_{i=1}^{n/a}V^{\ast}_{i}
is
\frac{n}{a}(q^{a}-1)
. On the other hand,
\frac{r_{1}}{(r_{1},\lambda_{1})}>q^{n/2}
by Theorem
5.1
(4). Therefore
q^{n/2}<\frac{n}{a}(q^{a}-1)
, which does not have admissible solutions for
n/a\geqslant 2
.

Assume that
G_{\Delta}^{\Sigma}
lies in a maximal member of one of the geometric classes
\mathcal{C}_{i}
of
N_{\Gamma L_{n}(q)}(X)
,
i=4,...,7
. Then
G_{\Delta}^{\Sigma}
is as in Table
7
.

Proof of Theorem
7.1
.

The group
\left(G_{\Delta}^{\Sigma}\right)^{(\infty)}
is a quasisimple group, and its action on
V_{n}(q)
is absolutely irreducible and not realizable over any proper subfield of
\mathbb{F}_{q}
. Set
W=G^{(\infty)}_{0}/Z(G^{(\infty)}_{0})
by Proposition
7.9
. Hence, we may adapt the aforementioned Liebeck’s argument to our context since

(7.9)

\frac{r_{1}}{(r_{1},\lambda_{1})}\mid(q^{n}-1,c_{1},...,c_{j},(q-1)\cdot|Aut(W)|)\text{~and~}\frac{r_{1}}{(r_{1},\lambda_{1})}>q^{n/2}\text{.}

by Theorem
5.1
(4)–(5), and we obtain that
\left(G_{\Delta}^{\Sigma}\right)^{(\infty)}
and the corresponding
v_{1}
is as in Tables
8
,
9

10
according
W
is alternating, sporadic, or of Lie type in natural characteristic, or of Lie type in cross characteristic, respectively. Moreover, since
G_{\Delta}^{\Sigma}\leqslant\left(Z_{q-1}\circ\left(G_{\Delta}^{\Sigma}\right)^{(\infty)}\right).Out(L)
, and
\lambda
does not divide the order of
Out(L)
by Lemma
7.4
(1), it follows that
\lambda
divides the order of
Z_{q-1}\circ\left(G_{\Delta}^{\Sigma}\right)^{(\infty)}
. Actually,
\lambda
divides the order of
\left(G_{\Delta}^{\Sigma}\right)^{(\infty)}
, and hence the order of
L
, since
\lambda\nmid v_{1}-1
being
\mathcal{D}_{1}
of type Ia.

Suppose that
W
is alternating or sporadic. As mentioned above
L
,
v_{1}
and
\frac{r_{1}}{(r_{1},\lambda_{1})}
are as in the second, third and fourth column of Table
8
, respectively, by adapting Liebeck’s argument. The entry in the fourth column is the greatest prime divisor of
L
greater than
3
and candidate to be
\lambda
. Now,
A=(v_{1}-1)\cdot\left(\frac{r_{1}}{(r_{1},\lambda_{1})}\right)^{-1}
by Proposition
3.5
(3). Except for that as in line 3, line 10 with
1\leqslant\theta<7
, line 11 with
(q,\theta,A)=(2,1,1),(2,2,2)
and
\lambda=7
, lines 13–14 with
\theta<5
, and those as in lines 15 or 16 with
2\leqslant p\leqslant 7
, all cases in Table
8
are such that
\lambda\leqslant\lambda_{\text{max}}\leqslant A
, and hence they are excluded by Proposition
3.5
(3).

In case as in line 3, one has
\lambda=5
since
\lambda_{\text{max}}=5
and
\lambda=3
. However, this case is excluded since
\frac{r_{1}}{(r_{1},\lambda_{1})}=\lambda=5
, contrary to Theorem
5.1
.

In case as in line 10, one has
\lambda=5
or
7
. Moreover,
\frac{r_{1}}{(r_{1},\lambda_{1})}=\frac{p^{2}-1}{\theta}>\lambda
implies
p^{2}>\lambda\theta+1
, and hence
v_{1}>\left(\lambda\theta+1\right)^{3}
. Also,
\frac{r_{1}}{(r_{1},\lambda_{1})}=\frac{v_{0}-1}{k_{0}-1}

leads
v_{0}>\lambda(k_{0}-1)+1
. So

\left(\lambda\theta+1\right)^{3}\left(\lambda(k_{0}-1)+1\right)<v\leqslant 2\lambda^{2}(\lambda-1)\text{,}

which is contrary to
\lambda=5
or
7
.

In case as in line 11, the action of
G^{\Sigma}
on
\mathcal{D}_{1}
is
either
2
-transitive or (primitive) rank
3
by
[
31
, Corolary 4.2]

according as
(q,\theta,A)=(2,1,1)
or
(q,\theta,A)=(2,2,2)
,
respectively. The former is excluded by Theorem
7.7
, the latter by
[
36
, Table 2]
.

In cases as in lines 13–14, one has
\lambda=5
,
\theta<5
. Moreover,
\frac{r_{1}}{(r_{1},\lambda_{1})}>\lambda
implies
\left(p-1\right)\cdot 2\cdot 6!\geqslant\left(p-1\right)\cdot 5!>\theta\lambda>25
which is
impossible.

Finally, in cases as in lines 15–16, one has
\lambda=5
and hence
p=2

or
3
since
v_{1}\leqslant v\leqslant 2\lambda^{2}(\lambda-1)
by
[
17
, Theorem 1]
. However, in both
cases, it results
\lambda\mid v_{1}-1
which is contrary to Theorem
3.9
since
\mathcal{D}_{1}
is of type Ia. Thus,
L
is neither alternating nor sporadic.

8.
Reduction to the case where
\mathcal{D}_{1}
is a
1
-symmetric design with
k_{1}=v_{1}-1

.

The aim of this section is to prove the following reduction theorem.

Theorem 8.1
.

Assume that Hypothesis
2.6
holds. Then
\mathcal{D}_{1}
is a
1
-symmetric design with
k_{1}=v_{1}-1
, and the parameters for
\mathcal{D}_{0}
,
\mathcal{D}_{1}
, and
\mathcal{D}
are those recorded in Table
1
by Lemma
3.3
.

To this end, we assume that
T=Soc(G^{\Sigma})
is non-abelian simple, which is the remaining case of Theorem
7.1
to be investigated when
\mathcal{D}_{1}
is a
2
-design. We analyze the cases where
T
is sporadic, alternating, a Lie type simple classical or exceptional group in sections.

Lemma 8.2
.

Assume that Hypothesis
3.12
holds. Then the following hold:

(1)

\left|T\right|\leqslant\left|T_{\Delta}\right|^{2}\cdot\left|Out(T)\right|
;

(2)

\lambda\mid\left|T_{\Delta}\right|
and
\lambda\mid\left|Out(T)\right|
;

(3)

T_{\Delta}
is a large subgroup of
T
.

Proof.

It follows from Theorem
7.1
(2) that
\left|T\right|\leqslant\left|T_{\Delta}\right|^{2}\cdot\left|Out(T)\right|
, which is (1).

Since
r_{1}=\frac{v_{0}-1}{k_{0}-1}\cdot\frac{v_{0}}{k_{0}\eta}\cdot\lambda
divides
\left|G^{\Sigma}_{\Delta}\right|
, and
\left|G^{\Sigma}_{\Delta}:T_{\Delta}\right|\mid\left|Out(T)\right|
, then either
\lambda\mid\left|T_{\Delta}\right|
or
\lambda\mid\left|Out(T)\right|
. Suppose that the latter occurs. Note that
v_{0}>\lambda+1
since
\frac{r_{1}}{(r_{1},\lambda_{1})}=\frac{v_{0}-1}{k_{0}-1}
by (
3.24
) and
\frac{r_{1}}{(r_{1},\lambda_{1})}>\lambda
by Theorem
7.1
. If
v_{1}\geqslant 2\lambda^{2}
, then
v=v_{1}v_{0}\geqslant 2\lambda^{2}(\lambda+1)
since
v_{0}>\lambda+1
,, but this contradicts
[
17
, Theorem 1]
. Thus,
P(T)\geqslant v_{1}<2\lambda^{2}
, where
P(T)
is the minimal degree
of the non-trivial transitive permutation representations of
T
. Hence,
T
is
neither alternating or sporadic since
\left|Out(T)\right|\leqslant 4

in these cases, whereas
\lambda
is a prime and
\lambda>3
.

Suppose that
T\cong PSL_{n}(q)
,
n\geqslant 2
, and
(n,q)\neq(2,2),(2,3)
.
Then
\lambda\mid(n,q-1)\cdot\log_{p}q
, and hence
(n,q)\neq(2,4),(2,5),(2,7),(2,9),(2,11)
or
(4,2)
since
\lambda
is a prime and
\lambda>3
. Thus,
P(T)=\frac{q^{n}-1}{q-1}
by
[
34
, Theorem 5.2.2]
.
If
\lambda\mid(n,q-1)
, then
\frac{q^{n}-1}{q-1}\leqslant 2(q-1)^{2}
and so
\lambda\leqslant n\leqslant 4
, a contradiction. Therefore
\lambda\mid\log_{p}q
,
and hence
\frac{q^{n}-1}{q-1}\leqslant 2q
since
\log_{p}q\leqslant q^{1/2}
.
Then
n=2
and
q+1\leqslant 2\log_{p}^{2}q
, which is impossible since
q>1
.

Suppose that
T\cong PSp_{n}(q)
,
n\geqslant 2
. We may assume that
n\geqslant 4
since the assertion follows fro
n=2
since
PSp_{2}(q)\cong PSL_{2}(q)
. Moreover,
q>2

and
(q,n)\neq(4,3)
otherwise
\left|Out(T)\right|\leqslant 2
, whereas
\lambda>3
. Then
P(T)=\frac{q^{n}-1}{q-1}
by
[
34
, Theorem 5.2.2]
, and we reach a contradiction as above. An entirely
similar proof the the previous one rules out
\Omega_{n}(q)
with
n
odd,
P\Omega_{n}^{\varepsilon}(2)
with
\varepsilon=\pm
,
U_{3}(5)
and
U_{n}(2)
.

Suppose that either
T\cong P\Omega_{n}^{\varepsilon}(q)
,
\varepsilon=\pm
and
q>2
and
n\geqslant 8
, or
T\cong PSU_{n}(q)
with
n,q\geqslant 3
and
(n,q)\neq(3,5)
. Then either
P(T)\geqslant\frac{(q^{n/2}+1)(q^{n/2-1}-1)}{q-1}

or
P(T)\geqslant q^{3}+1
, respectively. Therefore, either
\frac{(q^{n/2}+1)(q^{n/2-1}-1)}{q-1}\leqslant 2\log_{p}^{2}q\leqslant 2q
or
q^{3}+1\leqslant 2q
, respectively, and both inequalities do not have admissible
solutions.

Finally, if
T
is an exceptional Lie type simple group, then
P(T)
is provided in
[
54
,
55
,
56
]
and it is easy to see that no cases fulfill
P(T)\leqslant v_{1}\leqslant 2\lambda^{2}(\lambda-1)
. This proves (2).

Suppose that
\left|T_{\Delta}\right|\leqslant\left|Out(T)\right|
. Then
P(T)\leqslant\left|T:T_{\Delta}\right|\leqslant\left|Out(T)\right|^{2}
, which is (
6.2
), and hence
T\cong PSL_{n}(q)
with
(n,q)=(2,9)
or
(3,4)
.

Assume that
T\cong PSL_{2}(9)
. Then
v_{1}=6,10
or
15
by
[
13
]
and
\lambda=5
. Actually,
v_{1}=10
or
15
since
\frac{v_{0}-1}{k_{0}-1}>\lambda
. So
\lambda\mid v_{1}
, contrary to Theorem
3.9
since
\mathcal{D}_{1}
is of type Ia or Ic.

Finally, assume that
T\cong PSL_{3}(4)
. Then
\lambda=5
or
7
. Further,
v_{1}=21,56
or
120
by
[
13
]
since
v_{1}\leqslant\left|Out(T)\right|^{2}=144
. Then
3.9
since
\lambda\mid v_{1}(v_{1}-1)
and
v_{1}\neq\frac{1}{2}(\lambda-1)(\lambda^{2}-2)
, whereas
\mathcal{D}_{1}
is of type Ia or Ic.

∎

8.1.
Novelties.

An important tool in carrying out the following analysis is the notion of novelty: a maximal subgroup
M
of an almost simple group
A
is a
novel
maximal subgroup (or, simply, a
novelty
) if
M\cap Soc(A)
is non-maximal in
Soc(A)
. More information on novelties can be found in
[
7
,
34
,
59
]
.

8.2.
The case where
T
is sporadic

In this section, we assume that
T
is a sporadic simple group.

Lemma 8.3
.

Assume that Hypothesis
3.12
holds. If
T
is a sporadic group, then
T
acts
point-primitively on
\mathcal{D}_{1}
.

Proof.

Suppose the contrary. Hence,
T_{\Delta}
is non-maximal in
S
. On the
other hand,
G_{\Delta}^{\Sigma}
is maximal in
G^{\Sigma}
. Thus
G_{\Delta}^{\Sigma}
is a novelty, and hence
T
and
G_{\Delta}^{\Sigma}
are as in the first and second column of Table
11
, respectively, by
[
59
, Table 1]
and
[
60
, Section 4]
. Then
v_{1}=\left|G^{\Sigma}:G_{\Delta}^{\Sigma}\right|
as in the third column of Table
11
and
easy computations lead to the fourth column of the same table.

8.3.
The case where
T
is alternating

In this section, we assume that
T\cong A_{n}
,
n\geqslant 5
.

Lemma 8.5
.

Assume that Hypothesis
3.12
holds. If
T\cong A_{n}
,
n\geqslant 5
, then
v_{1}=n
and
\mathcal{D}_{1}
is the complete
2
-
\left(v_{1},k_{1},\binom{v_{1}-2}{k_{1}-2}\right)
design.

Proof.

Assume that
T\cong A_{n}
for some
n\geqslant 5
, and let
\Omega_{n}=\{1,...,n\}
. Now,
G_{\Delta}^{\Sigma}
is maximal subgroup of
G^{\Sigma}\cong A_{n}
or
S_{n}
, and both
G^{\Sigma}
and
G_{\Delta}^{\Sigma}
act on the point-set of
\mathcal{D}_{1}
as well as on
\Omega_{n}
. We distinguish three cases according as the action of
G_{\Delta}^{\Sigma}
on
\Omega_{n}
is primitive, imprimitive or intransitive. In the first case, we use
[
1
, Theorem 2]
or
[
61
, Lemma 2.6]
according as
G^{\Sigma}\cong A_{n}
or
S_{n}
, respectively, since
\left|G^{\Sigma}\right|\leqslant\left|G_{\Delta}^{\Sigma}\right|^{2}
by Theorem
7.1
(2), and we obtain Table
14
. In each case, one has
\lambda\mid v_{1}(v_{1}-1)
and
v_{1}\neq\frac{1}{2}\left(\lambda-1\right)\left(\lambda^{2}-2\right)
, and hence they are excluded by Theorem
3.9
since
\mathcal{D}_{1}
is of type Ia or Ic by Theorem
7.1
(1). Therefore,
G_{\Delta}^{\Sigma}
acts on
\Omega_{n}
either imprimitively or intransitively.

Assume that

. Let

-invariant partition of

,

,

and

. Hence, we may identify the point-set of

(or, as in

]
). Moreover, the
\Theta_{j}
-cyclic partitions with respect (a partition of

into

-orbits of
\mathcal{D}_{1}
distinct from

for

]
for definitions and details). Then

(8.2)

v_{1}=\frac{1}{t!}\prod_{j=1}^{t}\binom{js}{s}=\prod_{j=1}^{t}\binom{js-1}{s-1}

If

, then
t\geqslant 3
and hence

(8.3)

\displaystyle v_{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\prod_{j=1}^{t}(2j-1)

\displaystyle\theta_{j}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left|\Theta_{j}\right|=\frac{1}{2}\binom{t}{j}\binom{2}{1}^{j}=2^{j-1}\binom{t}{j}\text{.}

Then
\frac{r_{1}}{(r_{1},\lambda_{1})}\mid t(t-1)
since
\frac{r_{1}}{(r_{1},\lambda_{1})}\mid\theta_{2}

(4) and

. Then

\prod_{j=1}^{t}(2j-1)=v_{1}<\left(\frac{r_{1}}{(r_{1},\lambda_{1})}\right)^{2}\leqslant t^{2}(t-1)^{2}

or

. Then either

.

If

, then
\frac{r_{1}}{(r_{1},\lambda_{1})}
divides

for each

, and hence
\frac{r_{1}}{(r_{1},\lambda_{1})}\mid s^{2}\binom{t}{2}^{2}
since
\theta_{2}=s^{2}\binom{t}{2}^{2}
. Then

2^{(s-1)(t-1)}<v_{1}<\left(\frac{r_{1}}{(r_{1},\lambda_{1})}\right)^{2}\leqslant s^{4}\binom{t}{2}^{4}\text{,}

which is (2) of
[
53
, p.5]
. Thus
t<6
or

, and we get

as in

, which are recorded in Columns 2 and 7 of Table

. All cases, but those as in Lines 14, 20 and 26 of Table

, contradict

since

by Theorem

(4), and so they are ruled out by Theorem

(3). Note that,

, or

, and hence they are excluded by Theorem

since

Finally, assume that
G_{\Delta}^{\Sigma}
acts intransitively on
\Omega_{n}
. Then
G_{\Delta}^{\Sigma}=\left(S_{s}\times S_{n-s}\right)\cap G^{\Sigma}
with
s<n/2
. Indeed, the case where
G_{\Delta}^{\Sigma}
has two orbits of equal size
n/2
on
\Omega_{n}
is ruled out by the maximality of
G_{\Delta}^{\Sigma}
in
G^{\Sigma}
, because the stabilizer of such a partition of
\Omega_{n}
would be between
G_{\Delta}^{\Sigma}
and
G^{\Sigma}
. The flag-transitivity of
G^{\Sigma}
implies that
G_{\Delta}^{\Sigma}
is transitive on the blocks
\mathcal{D}_{1}
through
\{\Delta\}
, and so
G_{\Delta}^{\Sigma}
fixes exactly one point in
\mathcal{D}_{1}
. Since
\mathcal{D}_{1}
-stabilizes only one
s
-subset of
\Omega_{n}
, we can identify the point set of
\mathcal{D}_{1}
with the set of all
s
-subsets of
\Omega_{n}
. So
v_{1}
and subdegrees of
G^{\Sigma}
are:

\displaystyle v_{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\binom{n}{s}

\displaystyle\psi_{i}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left|\Psi_{i}\right|=\binom{s}{i}\binom{n-s}{s-i}

Then
\frac{r_{1}}{(r_{1},\lambda_{1})}\mid s(n-s)
since
\frac{r_{1}}{(r_{1},\lambda_{1})}\mid\psi_{1}
and
\psi_{1}=s(n-s)
. Then

(8.4)

\binom{n}{s}=v_{1}<\left(\frac{r_{1}}{(r_{1},\lambda_{1})}\right)^{2}\leqslant s^{2}(n-s)^{2}\text{,}

which is (4) of
[
53
, p.6]
, and hence
s\leqslant 6
. Moreover, if
s\geqslant 3
then one of the following holds:
s=3
and
7\leqslant n\leqslant 50
,
s=4
and
9\leqslant n\leqslant 18
,
s=5
and
11\leqslant n\leqslant 14
, or
s=6
and
n=13
. Now, exploiting
\frac{r_{1}}{(r_{1},k_{1})}\mid\left(s(n-s),\binom{n}{s}-1\right)
and (
8.4
) one obtains Table
16
.

For each value of
v_{1}
and
r_{1}/(r_{1},\lambda_{1})
as in Table
16
, we determine
A=(v_{1}-1)/\left(r_{1}/(r_{1},\lambda_{1})\right)
by Proposition
3.5
(3) since
r_{1}/(r_{1},\lambda_{1})=\frac{v_{0}-1}{k_{0}-1}
by (
3.24
). Except for the case as in line 3 with
\lambda=13
, we have
\lambda\leqslant\lambda_{\text{max}}\leqslant A
, and this is contrary to Proposition
3.5
(4). In the remaining case, one has
k_{0}=2
again Proposition
3.5
(4) since
k_{0}\geqslant 2
. Then
v_{0}=34
, and hence
k_{1}=A\frac{v_{0}}{k_{0}}+1=2^{2}\cdot 47
by Proposition
3.5
(3). However, this is impossible since
k_{1}
does not divide the order of
G^{\Sigma}
, being
G^{\Sigma}\leqslant S_{n}
, which is contrary to
G^{\Sigma}
flag-transitive on
\mathcal{D}_{1}
.

If
s=2
then
v_{1}=\frac{n(n-1)}{2}
and
\frac{r_{1}}{(r_{1},\lambda_{1})}=\frac{2(n-2)}{\alpha}
for some positive integer
\alpha
, and hence

(8.5)

\frac{(n+1)(n-2)}{2}=v_{1}-1<\left(\frac{r_{1}}{(r_{1},\lambda_{1})}\right)^{2}=\frac{4(n-2)^{2}}{\alpha^{2}}\text{.}

Thus
\alpha=1,2
. Arguing as above we see that
A=\frac{v_{1}-1}{r_{1}/(r_{1},\lambda_{1})}=\frac{(n+1)\alpha}{4}
. Then
\lambda\geqslant\frac{(k_{0}-1)\alpha}{4}(n+1)+1
by Proposition
3.5
(4). Since
\lambda
divides
k_{1}
, and
A_{n}\unlhd G^{\Sigma}\leqslant S_{n}
acts flag-transitively on
\mathcal{D}_{1}
, it follows that
\lambda\mid n!
. Thus
\lambda\leqslant n
, and hence either
\alpha=k_{0}=2
and
n
odd, or
\alpha=1
,
n\equiv 3\pmod{4}
and
k_{0}=2,3,4
.

Assume that
\alpha=k_{0}=2
and
n
is odd. Then
A=v_{0}-1=\frac{n+1}{2}
and
v_{0}=\frac{n+3}{2}
, and hence
k_{1}=A\frac{v_{0}}{k_{0}}+1=\frac{1}{8}(n^{2}+4n+11)
with
n\equiv 1\pmod{4}
by Proposition
3.5
(4). Then
n=5
since
v_{1}<2k_{1}
and
n\geqslant 5
, and hence
k_{1}=\lambda=7
, which is a contradiction.

Assume that
\alpha=1
,
n\equiv 3\pmod{4}
and
k_{0}=2,3,4
. Then
A=\frac{n+1}{4}
and
v_{0}=\frac{n+1}{4}(k_{0}-1)+1
, and hence

(8.6)

k_{1}=\frac{n+1}{4}\left(\frac{n+1}{4}(k_{0}-1)+1\right)\frac{1}{k_{0}}+1\text{.}

Since
v_{1}<2k_{1}
, it follows that

\frac{n(n-1)}{2}<\frac{n+1}{2}\left(\frac{n+1}{4}(k_{0}-1)+1\right)\frac{1}{k_{0}}+2\text{,}

which leads to no cases since
n\geqslant 5
.

Finally, assume that
s=1
. Hence
v_{1}=n
. Therefore,
G^{\Sigma}
acts point-
(v_{1}-2)
-transitively on
\mathcal{D}_{1}
. Then
G^{\Sigma}
acts
k_{1}
-transitively since
2<k_{1}<v_{1}-1
, and hence
b_{1}=\binom{v_{1}}{k_{1}}
, where
b_{1}
is the number of blocks of
\mathcal{D}_{1}
. Now, we deduce from
b_{1}k_{1}=v_{1}r_{1}
and
\lambda_{1}(v_{1}-1)=r_{1}(k_{1}-1)
that
r_{1}=\binom{v_{1}-1}{k_{1}-1}
and
\lambda_{1}=\binom{v_{1}-2}{k_{1}-2}
. This completes the proof.
∎

Lemma 8.6
.

Assume that Hypothesis
3.12
holds. Then
T
is not an alternating group.

Proof.

Suppose that
T\cong A_{n}
,
n\geqslant 5
. Then
v_{1}=n
and
\mathcal{D}_{1}
is the complete
2
-
\left(v_{1},k_{1},\binom{v_{1}-2}{k_{1}-2}\right)
design by Lemma
8.5
. Assume that
G_{(\Sigma)}=1
. Then
G
acts faithfully on
\Sigma
, and
hence
A_{v_{1}-1}\trianglelefteq G_{\Delta}\leqslant S_{v_{1}-1}
. If
A_{v_{1}-1}\trianglelefteq G_{(\Delta)}
, then
G_{\Delta}^{\Delta}\leqslant Z_{2}
and so
v_{0}\leqslant 2
since
G_{\Delta}^{\Delta}
acts
point-transitively on
\mathcal{D}_{0}
, whereas
v_{0}>3
by Lemma
3.1
. Thus
A_{v_{1}-1}\trianglelefteq G_{\Delta}^{\Delta}\leqslant S_{v_{1}-1}
.
If
k_{0}=2
, then
G_{\Delta}^{\Delta}
acts point-
2
-transitively on
\mathcal{D}_{0}
, and hence either
v_{0}=v_{1}-1
and
v=v_{1}(v_{1}-1)
,
or
v_{0}=15
and
v_{1}-1=7
. In the former case, we have
A_{8}\trianglelefteq G\leqslant S_{8}
and so
\lambda=5
or
7
since
\lambda

is a prime divisor of
\left|G\right|
and
\lambda>3
. Then
\lambda\mid

v_{0}(v_{1}-1)
and
v_{1}\neq\frac{1}{2}(\lambda-1)(\lambda^{2}-2)
, and this contradicts Theorem
3.9
since
\mathcal{D}_{1}
is of type Ia or Ic by Theorem
7.1
(1). Thus,
v_{0}=v_{1}-1
and
v=v_{1}(v_{1}-1)
. Then
A=1
since
k_{1}<v_{1}
with
k_{1}=A\frac{v_{1}-1}{2}+1
by Proposition
3.5
(3). So
k_{1}=\frac{v_{1}+1}{2}
, ad hence
k=v_{1}+1
. Then
(v-1,k-1)=(v_{1}(v_{1}-1)-1,v_{1})=1
and hence
G
acts point-primitively on

\mathcal{D}
by
[
31
, Corollary 4.2]
, but this is contrary to our
assumptions. Thus
k_{0}>2
. If
\mu=1
, then
\mathcal{D}_{0}
is
2
-
(v_{1}-1,k_{0},\lambda)
design admitting
A_{v_{1}-1}\trianglelefteq G_{\Delta}^{\Delta}\leqslant S_{v_{1}-1}
as a flag-transitive automorphism
group, but this is contrary to Theorem
[
64
, Theorem 1]
. Therefore,
\mu=\lambda
by Lemma
2.7
(1), and hence
\mathcal{D}_{0}
is
2
-
(v_{1}-1,k_{0},1)
design admitting
A_{v_{1}-1}\trianglelefteq G_{\Delta}^{\Delta}\leqslant S_{v_{1}-1}
as a flag-transitive automorphism group, which
is not the case by
[
9
, Theorem]
. Therefore
G_{(\Sigma)}\neq 1
,
and hence
\eta=\frac{v_{0}}{k_{0}}
.

Then

(8.7)

\binom{v_{1}-2}{v_{1}-k_{1}}=\binom{v_{1}-2}{k_{1}-2}=\lambda_{1}=\frac{v_{0}}{k_{0}}\cdot\lambda\text{.}

On the other hand,
k_{1}=A\frac{v_{0}}{k_{0}}+1
and
\lambda\mid k_{1}
by Propositions
3.5
(3) and
4.6
. Thus,
\frac{v_{0}}{k_{0}}\cdot\lambda\leqslant k_{1}^{2}
, and hence

(8.8)

\sum_{j=0}^{v_{1}-k_{1}}\binom{k_{1}-3+j}{j}=\binom{k_{1}-3+(v_{1}-k_{1})+1}{v_{1}-k_{1}}\leqslant k_{1}^{2}\text{.}

If
v_{1}-k_{1}\geqslant 3
, then (
8.8
) leads to
k_{1}^{2}-6k_{1}-1\leqslant 0
, and hence to
k_{1}\leqslant 6
. Actually,
k_{1}=\lambda=5
since
\lambda\mid k_{1}
and
\lambda>3
. Moreover,
v_{1}=8
or
9
since
v_{1}-k_{1}\geqslant 3
and
v_{1}<2k_{1}
. Then
A\frac{v_{0}-1}{k_{0}-1}=7
or
8
, respectively. In each case, we have
A=1
and
\frac{v_{0}-1}{k_{0}-1}=7
or
8
, respectively, since
\frac{v_{0}-1}{k_{0}-1}>\lambda=5
, and hence
\frac{v_{0}}{k_{0}}=4
since
k_{1}=5
. So,
\frac{4k_{0}-1}{k_{0}-1}=7
or
8
, and hence only the former occurs which implies
k_{0}=2
and
v_{0}=8
. Then
v=56
and
k=10
, and hence
(v-1,k-1)=1
, and so
G
acts point-primitively on

\mathcal{D}
by
[
31
, Corollary 4.2]
, but this is contrary to our
assumptions.

If
v_{1}-k_{1}<3
, then
v_{1}-k_{1}=1
or
2
since
k_{1}<v_{1}
. Therefore,
k_{1}-1=\frac{v_{0}}{k_{0}}\lambda
or
\frac{1}{2}k_{1}(k_{1}-1)=\frac{v_{0}}{k_{0}}\lambda
, respectively. The former is ruled out, whereas the latter yields
\frac{k_{1}}{\lambda}(k_{1}-1)=2\frac{v_{0}}{k_{0}}
, since
\lambda\mid k_{1}
. Then either
A=1
and
k_{1}=2\lambda
, or
A=2
and
k_{1}=\lambda
since
k_{1}=A\frac{v_{0}}{k_{0}}+1
by Proposition
3.5
(3). The latter implies
\lambda=2\frac{v_{0}}{k_{0}}+1>\frac{v_{0}-1}{k_{0}-1}
since
v_{0}>3
which is contrary to Theorem
7.1
. Thus,
A=1
and
k_{1}=2\lambda
. Then
2\lambda=k_{1}=\frac{v_{0}}{k_{0}}+1
and
2\lambda+2=k_{1}+2=v_{1}=\frac{v_{0}-1}{k_{0}-1}+1
implies

\left(\frac{v_{0}}{k_{0}}+1\right)\frac{1}{2}=\left(\frac{v_{0}-1}{k_{0}-1}+1\right)\frac{1}{2}-1\text{.}

Easy computations show that
v_{0}=k_{0}\left(2k_{0}-1\right)
, which substituted in
2\lambda=\frac{v_{0}}{k_{0}}+1
leads to
\lambda=k_{0}
. Then
\mathcal{D}_{1}
is neither of type Ia nor of type Ic by
Theorem
3.9
, which is not the case by Theorem
7.1
(1). Thus,
T
cannot be
A_{n}
with
n\geqslant 5
.

∎

8.4.
The case where
T
is an exceptional Lie type simple group

In this section, we assume that
T
is an exceptional group of Lie type

Lemma 8.7
.

Assume that Hypothesis
3.12
holds. If
T
is an exceptional group of Lie type, then
T
acts point-primitively on
\mathcal{D}_{1}
and one of the following holds:

(1)

T\cong

Sz(q)
,
q=2^{2h+1}\geqslant 8
,
v_{1}=q^{2}+1
and
T_{\Delta}\cong[q^{2}]:Z_{q-1}
;

(2)

T\cong

{}^{2}G_{2}(q)^{\prime}
,
q=3^{2h+1}\geqslant 27
,
v_{1}=q^{3}+1
and
T_{\Delta}\cong[q^{3}]:Z_{q-1}
.

Proof.

Assume that
T
is an exceptional group of Lie type. It follows from Theorem
7.1
(2) that
G_{\Delta}^{\Sigma}
is a large maximal subgroup
of
G^{\Sigma}
. Suppose that
G_{\Delta}^{\Sigma}
is a not a parabolic
subgroup of
G^{\Sigma}
. Then the possibilities for the pairs
(T,T_{\Delta})
, where
T_{\Delta}=G_{\Delta}^{\Sigma}\cap T
, are
listed in
[
2
, Theorem 1.6]
. For each of these cases it is easy to
compute
v_{1}=\left|T:T_{\Delta}\right|
since
G^{\Sigma}

acts point-primitively on
\mathcal{D}_{1}
and
T\trianglelefteq G^{\Sigma}
. Although the
2
-
(v^{\prime},k^{\prime},\lambda^{\prime})

designs
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
with
r^{\prime}=\frac{(v^{\prime}-1)\lambda^{\prime}}{k^{\prime}-1}
admitting a flag-transitive
automorphism group
\Gamma^{\prime}
investigated in
[
3
]
are
different from our
\mathcal{D}_{1}
, nevertheless we can apply to our case some purely group-theoretical
results contained in
[
3
]
. Firstly,
note that listed given in
[
2
, Theorem 1.6]
is the same as in
[
3
, Tables 2 and 3]
. Now, the lower bound for
\left|T:T_{\Delta}\right|
given in
[
3
, Table 2]
is a lower bound for our
v_{1}
,
that we denote by
\ell_{v_{1}}
. Hence
\ell_{v_{1}}=\ell_{v^{\prime}}
, where
\ell_{v^{\prime}}
is as in the third column of
[
3
, Table 2]
. As pointed out in
[
3
, p. 1009]
, the upper bound
u_{r^{\prime}}

for
r^{\prime}
recorded in the last column
[
3
, Table 2]
is obtained
as a divisor of
(v^{\prime}-1,\left|\Gamma_{x^{\prime}}^{\prime}\right|)
with
x^{\prime}
any fixed point of
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}

the stabilizer
[
3
, Table 2]
, in some cases together with the knowledge
of some subdegrees of
\Gamma^{\prime}
. In our context the roles of
\Gamma^{\prime}
and
r^{\prime}
are played by
G^{\Sigma}
and
\frac{r_{1}}{(r_{1},\lambda_{1})}
, respectively, since
\frac{r_{1}}{(r_{1},\lambda_{1})}\mid(v_{1}-1,\left|G_{\Delta}^{\Sigma}\right|)
by Theorem
7.1
(4). Therefore,
u_{r^{\prime}}=u_{r_{1}/(r_{1},\lambda_{1})}
for each case listed in
[
3
, Table 2]
. So
\left(\frac{r_{1}}{(r_{1},\lambda_{1})}\right)^{2}\leqslant u_{r_{1}/(r_{1},\lambda_{1})}^{2}<\ell_{v_{1}}\leqslant v_{1}
for each case as
in
[
3
, Table 2]
, and hence the possibilities for
(T,T_{\Delta})

as in
[
3
, Table 2]
are excluded since they contradict Theorem
7.1
(3). Thus,
(T,T_{\Delta})
is as one of the possibilities provided in
[
3
, Table 3]
. By Lemma
8.2
(1), we may filter such pairs
(T,T_{\Delta})
with respect
v_{1}=\left|T:T_{\Delta}\right|\leqslant\left|T_{\Delta}\right|\cdot\left|Out(T)\right|
, and we see that only the cases as in Table
17

are admissible (the lines 2–5 of
[
3
, Table 3]
actually do
not occur since the Ree group exist only for odd powers of
3
):

Since
\frac{r_{1}}{(r_{1},\lambda_{1})}\mid\left(v_{1}-1,\left|T_{\Delta}\right|\cdot\left|Out(T)\right|\right)
by Theorem
7.1
(3), it follows that
\left(\frac{r_{1}}{(r_{1},\lambda_{1})}\right)^{2}<v_{1}
, which is contrary to Theorem
7.1
(3). Thus all cases as in Table
17
are excluded, and hence
G_{\Delta}^{\Sigma}
is a maximal parabolic subgroup of
G^{\Sigma}
.

Assume that
T
is isomorphic to
E_{6}(q)
. If
G_{\Delta}^{\Sigma}

=P_{3}
of typer
A_{1}\times A_{4}
, then
v_{1}=(q^{3}+1)(q^{4}+1)(q^{9}-1)(q^{12}-1)/(q^{2}-1)(q-1)
and
\frac{r_{1}}{(r_{1},\lambda_{1})}\mid q(q^{5}-1)(q-1)\log q
since
\frac{r_{1}}{(r_{1},\lambda_{1})}\mid\left(v_{1}-1,\left|G_{\Delta}^{\Sigma}\right|\right)
by Theorem
7.1
(4), and so
\left(\frac{r_{1}}{(r_{1},\lambda_{1})}\right)^{2}<v_{1}
, which is contrary to Theorem
7.1
(3). If
G_{\Delta}^{\Sigma}

=P_{1}
, then
\frac{r_{1}}{(r_{1},\lambda_{1})}

divides the subdegrees
q(q^{3}+1)\frac{q^{8}-1}{q-1}
and
q^{8}(q^{4}+1)\frac{q^{5}-1}{q-1}
, and hence
\left(\frac{r_{1}}{(r_{1},\lambda_{1})}\right)^{2}<v_{1}
, a contradiction (e.g see
[
48
, p.345]
). Then
G_{\Delta}^{\Sigma}
has a unique one point-orbit on
\mathcal{D}_{1}

which is a power of the characteristic of
T
by
[
48
, Lemma 2.6]
, and
hence
\frac{r_{1}}{(r_{1},\lambda_{1})}\mid\left(v_{1}-1\right)_{p}
since
\frac{r_{1}}{(r_{1},\lambda_{1})}\mid v_{1}-1
. At this point, if
T

is neither
Sz(q)
,
q=2^{2h+1}\geqslant 8
, nor
{}^{2}G_{2}(q)^{\prime}
,
q=3^{2h+1}\geqslant 2
, proceeding as in
[
3
, p. 1013]
, we see that
\left(v_{1}-1\right)_{p}^{2}<v_{1}
and hence no cases occurs. Therefore,
either
T\cong

Sz(q)
,
q=2^{2h+1}\geqslant 8
, and
T_{\Delta}\cong[q^{2}]:Z_{q-1}
and
v_{1}=q^{2}+1
, or
T\cong

{}^{2}G_{2}(q)^{\prime}
,
q=3^{2h+1}\geqslant 3
, and
T_{\Delta}\cong[q^{3}]:Z_{q-1}
and
v_{1}=q^{3}+1
. In both cases
T
acts primitively on
\mathcal{D}_{1}
by
[
7
, Tables 8.16 and 8.43]
. Moreover, if
T\cong

{}^{2}G_{2}(3)^{\prime}\cong PSL_{2}(8)
, then
\lambda=7
and
v_{1}=28
. However, this is impossible by Theorem
3.9
(1) since
\lambda\mid v_{1}
and
v_{1}\neq\frac{1}{2}
, because
\mathcal{D}_{1}
is of type Ia or Ic by Theorem
7.1
(1). Thus,
T\cong

{}^{2}G_{2}(q)
with
q\geqslant 27
. This completes the proof.
∎

Lemma 8.8
.

Assume that Hypothesis
3.12
holds. If
T
is not an exceptional group of Lie type, then

(1)

T\cong

Sz(q)
,
q=2^{2h+1}\geqslant 8
,
T_{\Delta}\cong[q^{2}]:Z_{q-1}
,
T_{B^{\Sigma}}\cong[q]:Z_{q-1}
;

(2)

T
acts flag-transitively on
\mathcal{D}_{1}
;

(3)

\lambda=q-1
is a Mersenne prime and the admissible parameters for the
2
-designs
\mathcal{D}_{0}
and
\mathcal{D}_{1}
are listed in Table
18
.

Table 18
.

2
-designs
\mathcal{D}_{0}
and

when
T
is the Suzuki group.

\frac{q-1}{\mu}

Proof.

Since
\frac{r_{1}}{(r_{1},\lambda_{1})}=\frac{v_{0}-1}{k_{0}-1}
,
v_{1}=A\frac{v_{0}-1}{k_{0}-1}+1
by (
3.24
) and Proposition
3.5
(3), and
v_{1}=q^{i}+1
with
i=2
or
3
according as
T
is
Sz(q)
or
{}^{2}G_{2}(q)
, respectively, it follows that

(8.9)

\frac{r_{1}}{(r_{1},\lambda_{1})}=\frac{v_{0}-1}{k_{0}-1}=\frac{q^{i}}{p^{s}}\text{\quad\text{ and } \quad}A=p^{s}\text{.}

Moreover,
k_{1}=p^{s}\frac{v_{0}}{k_{0}}+1
and

(8.10)

b_{1}=\frac{v_{1}r_{1}}{k_{1}}=\frac{(q^{i}+1)\cdot\frac{v_{0}-1}{k_{0}-1}\cdot\frac{v_{0}}{k_{0}\cdot\eta}\cdot\lambda}{p^{s}\frac{v_{0}}{k_{0}}+1}=\frac{(q^{i}+1)\cdot\frac{q^{i}}{p^{s}}\cdot\frac{v_{0}}{k_{0}\cdot\eta}\cdot\lambda}{p^{s}\frac{v_{0}}{k_{0}}+1}\text{.}

It follows from Theorem
3.9
that
\mathcal{D}_{1}
is not of type Ic since
v_{1}=q^{i}+1
with
i=2,3
. Therefore,
\mathcal{D}_{1}
is not of type Ia by Theorem
3.9
, and hence
\lambda\nmid v_{1}(v_{1}-1)
then
\mathcal{D}_{1}
by Theorem
3.9
. Moreover,
\lambda
divides the order of
T
since
\lambda
divides the order of
G^{\Sigma}
, and
\lambda
divides the order of
Out(T)
by Lemma
8.2
(1). Then
\lambda\mid q-1
since the order of
T
is
q^{i}(q^{i}+1)(q-1)
,with
i=2,3
.

Assume that
T\cong

{}^{2}G_{2}(q)
,
q=3^{2h+1}\geqslant 27
, and
T_{\Delta}\cong[q^{3}]:Z_{q-1}
and
v_{1}=q^{3}+1
. Then
\lambda\leqslant(q-1)/2
, and hence
[
17
, Theorem 1]
implies
q^{3}+1\leqslant(q-1)^{2}(q-3)/4
, a contradiction since
q>1
.

Assume that
T\cong

Sz(q)
,
q=2^{2h+1}\geqslant 8
, and
T_{\Delta}\cong[q^{2}]:Z_{q-1}
and
v_{1}=q^{2}+1
. Let
K=T_{B^{\Sigma}}
, where
B^{\Sigma}
is any block of
\mathcal{D}_{1}
and let
M
be any maximal subgroup of
T
containing
K
. Since
K
acts transitively on
B^{\Sigma}
, it follows that
k_{1}
divides the order of
K
, and hence
\lambda
divides the order of
K
since
\lambda\mid k_{1}
by Theorem
7.1
. Therefore,
\lambda\mid(\left|M\right|,q-1)
since
K\leqslant M
. Then
M
is either isomorphic to
[q^{2}]:Z_{q-1}
,
D_{2(q-1)}
, or to
Sz(q^{1/m})
with
m
a proper (odd) prime divisor of
\log_{p}(q)
by
[
7
, Table 8.16]
. In the latter case,
\lambda\mid q^{1/m}-1
since
\lambda\mid(\left|M\right|,q-1)
and
(q^{2}+1,q-1)=1
being
q
even. By
[
17
, Theorem 1]
, one has
q^{2}+1\leqslant v\leqslant 2\lambda^{2}(\lambda-1)\leqslant 2q^{1/m}(q^{2/m}-1)
with
m
a proper divisor of
\log_{2}(q)
, and we reach a contradiction. In the first two cases,
q^{2}+1
divides
\left|T:M\right|
and hence
b_{1}
. Thus
2^{s}\frac{v_{0}}{k_{0}}+1\mid\frac{q^{2}}{2^{s}}\cdot\frac{v_{0}}{k_{0}\cdot\eta}\cdot\lambda
by (
8.10
), and so
2^{s}\frac{v_{0}}{k_{0}}+1\mid\frac{q^{2}}{2^{s}}\cdot\lambda
. If
s>1
then
2^{s}\frac{v_{0}}{k_{0}}+1\mid\lambda
, and hence
k_{1}=\lambda
since
k_{1}=2^{s}\frac{v_{0}}{k_{0}}+1
and
\lambda\mid k_{1}
. So
q^{2}+1=v_{1}<2k_{1}=2\lambda\leqslant 2(q-1)
, a contradiction. Thus
s=1
and
\frac{v_{0}}{k_{0}}+1\mid q^{2}\cdot\lambda
. Therefore
A=1
, and hence
k_{1}=\frac{q^{2}}{2^{t}}\lambda
for some nonnegative integer
t
such that
\frac{2^{t}}{2}<\lambda<p^{t}
. Thus,
\frac{q^{2}}{2^{t}}\lambda>\frac{q^{2}+1}{2}
since
k_{1}>v_{1}/2
, and hence

\frac{q^{2}}{2^{t}}>\frac{q+1}{2}
since
\lambda\mid q-1
, forcing
q\mid k_{1}
since
q
is a power of
2
. Thus,
M\cong[q^{2}]:Z_{q-1}
.

Note that, any Sylow
p
-subgroup of
T
partitions the point set of
\mathcal{D}_{1}
into two orbits of length
1
and
q^{2}
. Thus the Sylow
p
-subgroup
W
of
K
acts semiregularly on
B^{\Sigma}
, and hence it has order
\frac{q^{2}}{2^{t}}
since
k_{1}=\frac{q^{2}}{2^{t}}\lambda
. Further, if
L
is any Sylow
\lambda
-subgroup of
K
, then
W:L
is a Frobenius group by
[
38
, Theorem IV.24.2.(c)]
. Then
\lambda\mid 2^{4h+2-t}-1
by
[
43
, Proposition 17.3(ii)]
, and hence
\lambda\mid 2^{(t,2h+1)}-1
since
\lambda\mid 2^{2h+1}-1
. Actually,
\lambda=2^{t}-1
since
2^{t-1}<\lambda<2^{t}
. and so
t
is a prime being
\lambda
a prime. If
t<2h+1
, then
3t\leqslant 2h+1
since
t\mid 2h+1
. Then
[
17
, Theorem 1]
implies

2^{4h+2}+1<2(2^{t}-1)^{2}(2^{t}-2)\text{,}

and so
4h+2<3t+1\leqslant 2h+2
, a contradiction. Thus
t=2h+1
, and hence
\lambda=q-1
and
k_{1}=q(q-1)
. Moreover,
K_{B^{\Sigma}}=W:L
acts regularly on
B^{\Sigma}
. Now,
K_{B^{\Sigma}}
partitions the point set of
\mathcal{D}_{1}
into three orbits of length
1
,
q
and
q(q-1)
, the last being the size of
B^{\Sigma}
. Thus
G_{B^{\Sigma}}^{\Sigma}=K:Z_{f}
where
f=\left|G^{\Sigma}:T\right|
, and hence
T
acts block-transitively on
\mathcal{D}_{1}
. Therefore,
T
acts flag-transitively on
\mathcal{D}_{1}
since
K
acts transitively on
B^{\Sigma}
. Then
b_{1}=\left|T:K\right|=q(q^{2}+1)
, and hence
r_{1}=q^{2}(q-1)
. Then
\eta=\frac{v_{0}}{k_{0}}=q^{2}-q-1
since
r_{1}=\frac{v_{0}-1}{k_{0}-1}\cdot\frac{v_{0}}{k_{0}\eta}\cdot\lambda
,
\frac{v_{0}-1}{k_{0}-1}=q^{2}
and
\frac{v_{0}}{k_{0}}=k_{1}-1
,
k_{1}=q(q-1)
and
\lambda=q-1
. Moreover,
\frac{v_{0}}{k_{0}}=q^{2}-q-1
and
\frac{v_{0}}{k_{0}}=q^{2}-q-1
imply

(8.11)

q^{2}(k_{0}-1)=(q^{2}-q-1)k_{0}-1

From (
8.11
) we derive
k_{0}=q-1
and
v_{0}=(q-1)(q^{2}-q-1)
. The admissible parameters of
\mathcal{D}_{0}
and
\mathcal{D}_{1}
are now those recorded in Table
18
. This completes the proof.
∎

Lemma 8.9
.

Assume that Hypothesis
3.12
holds. Then
T
is not an exceptional group of Lie type.

Proof.

We know that either
\mu=q-1
or
\mu=1
by Lemmas
2.7
(1) and
8.8
. If
\mu=q-1
, then
\mathcal{D}_{0}
is a
2
-
((q-1)(q^{2}-q-1),q-1,1)
design admitting
G^{\Delta}_{\Delta}
as a flag-transitive automorphism group again by Lemma
8.8
. Note that
v_{0}=(q-1)(q^{2}-q-1)
is an odd composite number, then one of the following holds by
[
9
, Theorem]
:

(1)

\mathcal{D}_{0}\cong PG_{m-1}(s)
,
m\geqslant 3
, and either
PSL_{m}(q)\trianglelefteq G_{\Delta}^{\Delta}\leqslant P\Gamma L_{m}(s)
,
or
(m,s)=(3,2)
and
G\cong A_{7}
;

(2)

\mathcal{D}_{0}
is the Hermitian unital of even order
s
, and
PSU_{3}(s)\trianglelefteq G_{\Delta}^{\Delta}\leqslant P\Gamma U_{3}(s)
.

Then we obtain either
\frac{s^{m}-1}{s-1}=(q-1)(q^{2}-q-1)
and
s+1=q-1
, or
s^{3}+1=(q-1)(q^{2}-q-1)
and
s+1=q-1
according as cases (1) or (2) occurs, respectively. Then either
s^{m}=s(s^{3}+3s^{2}-3)
with
m\geqslant 3
, or
s^{3}=s^{3}+4s(s+1)
, and any of these does not have admissible solutions.

Assume that
\mu=1
. Then
\mathcal{D}_{0}
is a
2
-
((q-1)(q^{2}-q-1),q-1,q-1)
design with
q-1
prime by Lemma
8.8
. Let
N
be the action kernel of
G_{B}
on
B
, then either
Z_{\lambda}\trianglelefteq G_{B}/N\leqslant AGL_{1}(\lambda)
and
G_{B}
acts
3/2
-transitively on
B
, or
G_{B}
acts
2
-transitively on
B
by
[
19
, p. 99]
. Then
G_{\Delta}^{\Delta}
acts either point-
3/2
-transitively or point-
2
-transitively on
\mathcal{D}_{0}
, respectively, since
rank(G_{B},B)=rank(G_{\Delta}^{\Delta},\Delta)
by Lemma
4.2
(1). Moreover,
G_{\Delta}^{\Delta}
is almost simple by
[
6
, Theorem 1.1]
since
v_{0}=(q-1)(q^{2}-q-1)
is a composite number, and hence
G_{\Delta}^{\Delta}
acts point-
2
-transitively on
\mathcal{D}_{0}
by

[
6
, Theorem 1.2]
since
v_{0}\neq 21
. Thus,
G
acts point-
2
-transitively on
\mathcal{D}
in any case. Then one of the following
holds by
[
32
, (A)]
since
v_{0}
is an odd composite integer not equal to
15
and
Soc(G_{\Delta}^{\Delta})\ncong A_{v_{0}}
by
[
64
, Theorem 1]
:

(i)

Soc(G_{\Delta}^{\Delta})\cong PSL_{m}(s)
,
m\geqslant 2
,
s=s_{0}^{e}
,
s_{0}
prime,
e\geqslant 1
and
v_{0}=\frac{s^{m}-1}{s-1}
;

(ii)

Soc(G_{\Delta}^{\Delta})\cong PSU_{3}(s)
,
s=2^{e}
,
e>2
,
and
v_{0}=s^{3}+1
;

(iii)

Soc(G_{\Delta}^{\Delta})\cong Sz(s)
,
s=2^{e}
,
e\geqslant 3
odd, and

v_{0}=s^{2}+1
.

Note that
v_{0}-1=q^{2}\left(q-2\right)
. Then
2^{et}=q^{2}\left(q-2\right)
with
t=3
or
2
in (ii) and (iii),
respectively, and so
q=4
which is not the case since
et\geqslant 6
. Thus (i) holds, and hence

(8.12)

2q^{2}\left(\frac{q}{2}-1\right)=s\frac{s^{m-1}-1}{s-1}\text{.}

Moreover, it is easy to see that
m>2
since
q\geqslant 8
is an odd power of
2
, and hence
s<q
.

Let
x,y
be any two distinct points of
\mathcal{D}_{0}
. Then
G^{\Delta}_{x,y}
acts transitively on the
\lambda
elements of
\mathcal{B}_{0}(x,y)
by Lemma
4.2
(2). It follows from
[
34
, Proposition 4.1.17(II) and Table 3.5.G]
,
[
19
, p.99]
and by (
8.12
) that, either
\lambda\mid(m,s-1)\cdot e
with
s_{0}^{e}=s
, or the group induced by
G^{\Delta}_{x,y}
on
\mathcal{B}_{0}(x,y)
contains
PSL_{u}(q)
as a normal subgroup and
\lambda=\frac{s^{u}-1}{s-1}
with
u\in\{2,m-2\}
. If
\lambda\mid(m,s-1)\cdot e
, then
\lambda\mid e
since
s<q
, and hence
\frac{s^{m}-1}{s-1}=v_{0}\leqslant s^{1/2}(s+s^{1/2}-1)
since
e\leqslant s^{1/2}
. So
m=2
, which we saw being impossible. Therefore
q-1=\lambda=\frac{s^{u}-1}{s-1}
with
u\in\{2,m-2\}
, and hence

(8.13)

\left(\frac{s^{u}-1}{s-1}+1\right)^{2}s\frac{s^{u-1}-1}{s-1}=s\frac{s^{m-1}-1}{s-1}

by (
8.12
). It is easy to see that there are no admissible solutions for
u=2
. So
u=m-2
, and hence
(m-4)+2(m-3)\leqslant m-1
, which leads to
m=3
since
m>2
. However, there are no admissible solutions for
m=3
. So this cases is excluded, and the proof is thus completed.
∎

8.5.
The case where
T
is a classical group

In this section, we assume that
T
is a classical group.

Lemma 8.10
.

Assume that Hypothesis
3.12
holds.
T_{\Delta}

lies in a maximal geometric subgroup of
T
.

Proof.

Assume that
T_{\Delta}
is an almost simple irreducible subgroup of the
classical group of
T
. Let
M
be a maximal subgroup of
T
containing
T_{\Delta}
. Then
M
is a large subgroup of
T
by Lemma
8.2
(2),
and hence the pair
(T,M)
is listed in
[
1
, Theorem 4(ii) and Table 7]
. We preliminary filter the pair
(T,M)
with respect
\left|T\right|^{2}\leqslant\left|M\right|\cdot\left|Out(T)\right|
by Lemma
8.2
(1) since
T_{\Delta}\leqslant M
, and we
see that no cases occur for
n>10
(note that, for
n>10
the cases are all
numerical). Thus
n\leqslant 10
, and by a direct inspection of
[
7
, Section 8]
we see that
G_{\Delta}^{\Sigma}
is not a novelty in
G^{\Sigma}

for any of the groups listed in
[
1
, Table 7]
, hence
T_{\Delta}=M
.
Thus
n\leqslant 10
and
(T,T_{\Delta})
are listed in
[
1
, Table 7]
. Now,
filtering such a list with respect
\left|T\right|^{2}\leqslant\left|T_{\Delta}\right|\cdot\left|Out(T)\right|
, the numerical cases with respect to
\left(\frac{r_{1}}{(r_{1},\lambda_{1})}\right)^{2}>v_{1}
and
\frac{r_{1}}{(r_{1},\lambda_{1})}\mid\left(v_{1}-1,\left|T_{\Delta}\right|\cdot\left|Out(T)\right|\right)
, and bearing in mind that

, we obtain the

Since

, and hence

or

, and

) and

. Then

. Thus

,

, and both cases are

with

∎

In the next proposition, the proof strategy used to obtain Table
20
is an adaptation of a group-theoretical argument used by Saxl in
[
48
]
(which is the analog for the almost simple groups of that of used for affine groups by Liebeck in
[
37
]
): the
2
-
(v^{\prime},k^{\prime},1)
designs
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
(linear spaces) admitting a flag-transitive group
T\unlhd H\leqslant Aut(T)
with
T
non abelian simple are such that the group
H_{x}
, are those fulfilling the following properties:

(8.14)

r^{\prime}\mid(v^{\prime}-1,c_{1},...,c_{j},\left|H_{x}\right|)\text{ and }r^{\prime}>(v^{\prime})^{1/2}\textit{,}

where
r^{\prime}=\frac{v^{\prime}-1}{k^{\prime}-1}
,
x
is any point of
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
, and
c_{1},...,c_{j}
are the lengths of the
H_{x}
-orbit on the set of points of
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
distinct from
\{x\}
by
[
48
, Lemma 2.1]
. In other words, in
[
48
]
, an almost simple group
H
is candidate to be a flag-transitive automorphism group of a linear space with
v^{\prime}
points if
H
admits a permutation representation of degree
v^{\prime}
with nontrivial subdegrees divisible by the same factor of size greater than square root of
v^{\prime}
. In our context, the constraints in (
8.14
) are replaced by

(8.15)

\frac{r_{1}}{(r_{1},\lambda_{1})}\mid(v_{1},c_{1},...,c_{j},\left|G_{\Delta}^{\Sigma}\right|)\text{~and~}\frac{r_{1}}{(r_{1},\lambda_{1})}>(v_{1})^{1/2}\text{,}

which hold by Theorem
7.1
(3)–(4) since
G^{\Sigma}
is allmost simple. Hence, we may use Saxl’s argument with
G^{\Sigma}
,
v_{1}
and
\frac{r_{1}}{(r_{1},\lambda_{1})}
in the role of
H
,
v^{\prime}
and
r^{\prime}
, respectively, to reduce our investigation to the cases where (
8.15
) is fulfilled. We did so, and we obtained as admissible cases those recorded in Table
20
.

Proposition 8.11
.

Assume that Hypothesis
3.12
holds. One of the following holds:

(1)

T\cong PSL_{n}(q)
,
n\geqslant 2
and
(n,q)\neq(2,2),(2,3)
;

(2)

T\cong Sp_{n}(2)
and
v_{1}=2^{n-1}(2^{n}+\varepsilon 1)
,
\varepsilon=\pm
.

Proof.

Assume that
T
is not isomorphic to
T\cong PSL_{n}(q)
,
n\geqslant 2
and
(n,q)\neq(2,2),(2,3)
. Recall that Table
20
consists of the admissible simple classical groups
T
with
T_{\Delta}
geometric fulfilling (
8.15
) admissible cases. Further
\lambda
is a prime,
\lambda\nmid v_{1}
by Theorems
3.9
since
\mathcal{D}_{1}
is of type Ia or Ic by Theorem
7.1
(1), and
\lambda\mid\left|T\right|
by Lemma
8.2
(2). Based on the previous constraints on
\lambda
for each of the cases as in Table
20
we determined an upper bound
u_{\lambda}
on the possible values of
\lambda
. It is a routine exercise to check that,
v_{1}\geqslant 2u_{\lambda}^{2}
for each case as in Table
20
, except for case as in lines 2 or 5. Therefore,
v_{1}\geqslant 2\lambda^{2}
for each case as in Table
20
, except for case as in lines 2 or 5, as
u_{\lambda}\geqslant\lambda
. Moreover,
v_{0}>\lambda+1
since
\frac{v_{0}-1}{k_{0}-1}>\lambda
by Theorem
7.1
(3) and (
3.24
) and
k_{0}\geqslant 2
. This leads to
v=v_{0}v_{1}>2\lambda^{2}(\lambda+1)
since
v_{1}\geqslant\lambda^{2}
, but this is contrary to
[
17
, Theorem 1]
. The same conclusion hold for the case as in line 2 of Table
20
when
\lambda\leqslant\frac{q^{2}+1}{2}
. Thus
T\cong PSp_{4}(q)
,
q
even,
v_{1}=\frac{q^{2}}{2}(q^{2}-1)
and
\lambda=q^{2}+1
. As pointed out in
[
48
, p.329]
, the subdegrees of
PSp_{4}(q)
,
q
even, in its primitive action of degree
\frac{q^{2}}{2}(q^{2}-1)
are one equal to
(q^{2}+1)(q-1)
and
\frac{q}{2}-1
ones equal to
q(q^{2}+1)
. Then
\frac{r_{1}}{(r_{1},\lambda_{1})}\mid q^{2}+1
by Theorem
7.1
(4), whereas
\frac{r_{1}}{(r_{1},\lambda_{1})}>\lambda=q^{2}+1
again by Theorem
7.1
. Thus, also the case as in line 2 of Table
20
is ruled out, and hence it remains the case as in line 5 to be analyzed. In this case
n\neq 2
since
Sp_{2}(q)\cong PSp_{2}(q)\cong PSL_{2}(q)
for
q
even contradicts our assumptions. Hence,
n\geqslant 4
. Moreover,
v_{1}<\leqslant 2u_{\lambda}^{2}
, otherwise we may use the previous argument to rule out this case. Hence, we have

\frac{q^{n/2}}{2}(q^{n/2}+\varepsilon 1)<2\left(\frac{q^{n/2}-\varepsilon 1}{q-\varepsilon 1}\right)^{2}\text{,}

from which we derive

(8.16)

(q-\varepsilon 1)^{2}<4\cdot\frac{q^{n/2}-\varepsilon 1}{q^{n/2}+\varepsilon 1}\cdot\frac{q^{n/2}-\varepsilon 1}{q^{n/2}}\text{.}

Thus either
\varepsilon=+
,
(q-\varepsilon 1)^{2}<4
and
q=2
, or
\varepsilon=-
,
(q+1)^{2}<8
since
n\geqslant 4
, and again
q=2
. Therefore
q=2
in any case when line 5 of Table
20
holds, and we obtain (2). This completes the proof.

Proposition 8.12
.

Assume that Hypothesis
3.12
holds.
T\cong PSL_{n}(q)
,
n\geqslant 2
and
(n,q)\neq(2,2),(2,3)
.

Proof.

Assume that
T\cong Sp_{n}(2)
,
T_{\Delta}\cong O_{n}^{\varepsilon}(2)
and
v_{1}=2^{n/2-1}(2^{n/2}+\varepsilon 1)
. We
are going to rule out this case in a series of steps, thus obtaining the
assertion by Lemma
8.11
. Assume that
\mathcal{D}_{1}
is of type Ic. Then

2^{n/2-1}\left(2^{n/2}+\varepsilon 1\right)=\frac{\lambda-1}{2}\cdot\left(\lambda^{2}-2\right)

by Theorem
3.9
, and hence
2^{n/2}\mid\lambda-1
and
\lambda^{2}-2\mid 2^{n/2}+\varepsilon 1
. So
\left(2^{n/2}+1\right)^{2}-2\leqslant 2^{n/2}+1
, which is impossible. Thus
\mathcal{D}_{1}
is of type Ia by
Theorem
7.1
, and hence
\lambda\nmid v_{1}(v_{1}-1)
.

Claim 1
.

n\geqslant 14
.

Note that,
n>4
since
Sp_{4}(2)^{\prime}\cong A_{6}
has been already
excluded in Lemma
8.6
. Further, if
n\leqslant 12
then
(n,\varepsilon,v_{1},\lambda)=(6,-,28,5)
,
(8,+,136,7)
,
(10,-,\allowbreak 496,\lambda)
with
\lambda=7,17
, or
(10,+\allowbreak,\allowbreak 528,\lambda)
with
\lambda=5,7
, or
(n,\varepsilon,v_{1},\lambda)
is either
(12,-,2016,\lambda)
with
\lambda=11,17
, or
(12,+,2080,\lambda)
with
\lambda=17,31
since
v_{1}=2^{n/2-1}(2^{n/2}+\varepsilon 1)
and
\lambda\nmid v_{1}(v_{1}-1)
. Now, for each divisors
\alpha
of
v_{1}-1
such that
\alpha>\lambda
and
\alpha^{2}>v_{1}
we compute the admissible
A(\alpha)=\frac{v-1}{\alpha}
. Further, the admissible
v_{0}
are given by
v_{0}(\alpha)=\alpha(k_{0}-1)+1
and hence
k_{0}\mid\alpha-1
since
k_{0}\mid v_{0}
. Hence,
for each divisor
\beta
of
\alpha-1
such that
1<\beta<\lambda
, we
compute
v_{0}(\alpha,\beta)=\alpha(\beta-1)+1
and
k_{1}(\alpha,\beta)=A\frac{v_{0}}{\beta}+1
with
v_{1}/2<k_{1}(\alpha,\beta)<v_{1}
. Therefore, the admissible parameters for
\mathcal{D}_{0}
and
\mathcal{D}_{1}
are recorded in Table
21
.

However, only case as in line 1 of Table
21
is admissible since
\lambda\mid k_{1}
by Theorem
7.1
and

by
[
17
, Theorem 1]
. In this case,
T\cong Sp_{6}(2)
and
T_{\Delta}\cong O_{6}^{+}(2)\cong S_{8}
, and hence
T_{x}\cong S_{6}\times Z_{2}
, which is not divisible
r=135
. Thus
n>12
, and hence
n\geqslant 14

since
n
is even.

Claim 2
.

If
G_{(\Sigma)}\neq 1
, then
T_{B^{\Sigma}}
is a large subgroup of
T
.

Easy computations show that,
n(n+1)/2-1>2n-2+4\log(2^{n}+1)
since
n\geqslant 14
. Moreover,
\left|T\right|>2^{n(n+1)/2-1}
by
[
1
, Corollary 4.3]
.
Therefore,

\left|T\right|>2^{n(n+1)/2-1}>2^{2n-4}(2^{n}+1)^{4}\geqslant 2^{2n-4}(2^{n}+\varepsilon 1)^{2}=v_{1}^{4}>b_{1}^{2}.

since
b_{1}\mid v_{1}(v_{1}-1)
by Lemma
6.1
(2). Then

\left|T_{B^{\Sigma}}\right|^{2}\geqslant\frac{\left|T\right|^{2}}{b_{1}^{2}}>\left|T\right|\frac{\left|T\right|}{b_{1}^{2}}>\left|T\right|

and hence
T_{B^{\Sigma}}
is a large subgroup of
T
.

Claim 3
.

Let
M
be a maximal
subgroup of
T
containing
T_{B^{\Sigma}}
. If
G_{(\Sigma)}\neq 1
, then
M\in\mathcal{C}_{1}(T)
.

Assume that
G_{(\Sigma)}\neq 1
. Let
M
be a maximal
subgroup of
T
containing
T_{B^{\Sigma}}
. Then
M
is a large subgroup of
T
by Claim 2, and hence
M
is one of the groups
listed in
[
1
, Proposition 4.22 and Table 7]
. In particular
M
is
geometric since
\left|M\right|^{2}>\left|T\right|
and
n\geqslant 2
. Moreover, since
\left|T:M\right|
divides
b_{1}
and
hence
v_{1}(v_{1}-1)=2^{n/2-1}(2^{n}-1)\left(2^{n/2-1}+\varepsilon 1\right)
it follows that
M
is divisible by a primitive prime divisor of
2^{n-2}-1
or
2^{n-4}-1
according as
\varepsilon=-
or
\varepsilon=+
,
respectively, since
n\geqslant 14
. Therefore, either
M\in\mathcal{C}_{1}(T)\cup\mathcal{C}_{8}(T)
or
M
is a
\mathcal{C}_{3}
-subgroup of type
Sp_{n/2}(4)
by
[
1
, Proposition 4.22]
and
[
34
, Proposition 4.3.7(II)]
. In the latter case,
2^{\frac{1}{8}n^{2}-1}
divides
\left|T:M\right|
, and hence divides
v_{1}(v_{1}-1)
, by
[
34
, Proposition 4.3.10(II)]
. Therefore
\frac{1}{8}n^{2}-1\leqslant n/2-1
, which is contrary to
n\geqslant 14
.

Assume that
M\in\mathcal{C}_{8}(T)
. Then
M\cong O_{n}^{\varepsilon^{\prime}}(2)
by
[
34
, Proposition 4.8.6(II)]
, and hence
\left|T:M\right|=2^{n/2-1}(2^{n/2}+\varepsilon^{\prime}1)
. Suppose that
T_{B^{\Sigma}}<M
. Then
\left|M:T_{B^{\Sigma}}\right|\geqslant P(M^{\prime})
, where
M^{\prime}\cong\Omega_{n}^{\varepsilon^{\prime}}(2)
and
P(M^{\prime})
is the minimum of the non-trivial transitive permutation degree of
M^{\prime}
. Then either
\left|M:T_{B^{\Sigma}}\right|\geqslant 2^{n/2}(2^{n/2-1}-1)
or
\left|M:T_{B^{\Sigma}}\right|>2^{n/2-1}(2^{n/2}-1)
according as
\varepsilon^{\prime}=-
or
\varepsilon^{\prime}=+
occurs, respectively, by
[
34
, Proposition 5.2.1(i) and Theorem 5.2.2]
. In each case, we have

\left|T:T_{B^{\Sigma}}\right|\geqslant 2^{n/2-1}(2^{n/2}+\varepsilon^{\prime}1)\cdot 2^{n/2}(2^{n/2-1}-1)\leqslant 2^{n-1}(2^{n/2}-1)(2^{n/2-1}-1)\text{.}

If
b_{1}\leqslant v_{1}(v_{1}-1)/2
, then
\left|T:T_{B^{\Sigma}}\right|\leqslant v_{1}(v_{1}-1)/2
and hence

2^{n-1}(2^{n/2}-1)(2^{n/2-1}-1)\leqslant\left|T:T_{B^{\Sigma}}\right|\leqslant 2^{n/2-2}(2^{n/2}+1)\left(2^{n/2-1}(2^{n/2}+1)-1\right)

forcing
n\leqslant 4
, a contradiction. Thus,
b_{1}=v_{1}(v_{1}-1)
,
A=1
and
k_{1}=\lambda

since
b_{1}\cdot\frac{k_{1}}{\lambda}=\frac{v_{1}(v_{1}-1)}{A}
by Lemma
6.1
(2). Now,
\lambda\mid\left|T_{\Delta}\right|
since
\lambda\nmid\left|Out(T)\right|
by Lemma
8.2
(2) and
\lambda\nmid v_{1}
. Thus
\lambda\mid 2^{n-2i}-1
with
i>1
or
\lambda\mid 2^{n/2-1}-\varepsilon 1
since with
T_{\Delta}\cong O_{n}^{\varepsilon}(2)
and
\lambda\nmid v_{1}-1
. In both cases,
\lambda\leqslant 2^{n-4}-1
since
n\geqslant 14
. Then

2^{n/2-1}(2^{n/2}+\varepsilon 1)=v_{1}<2k_{1}=2\lambda<2^{n-3}\text{,}

which is clearly impossible. Thus, we have
T_{B^{\Sigma}}=M\cong O_{n}^{\varepsilon^{\prime}}(2)
.

If
\varepsilon^{\prime}=\varepsilon
, then
r_{1}=k_{1}
and hence
\lambda l^{m-w}+1<\frac{l^{m}-1}{l^{w}-1}\lambda=Al^{m-h}+1
since
v_{0}=l^{m}
and
k_{0}=l^{w}
with
l
prime and
1\leqslant w\leqslant m
by Lemma
6.1
(1).
So
A>\lambda
, which is contrary to Proposition
3.5
(4). Therefore, we obtain
\varepsilon^{\prime}=-\varepsilon
. Then
b_{1}=2^{n/2-1}(2^{n/2}-\varepsilon 1)
and
v_{1}=2^{n/2-1}(2^{n/2}+\varepsilon 1)
, and hence
\varepsilon=-
since
v_{1}\leqslant b_{1}
. Thus
b_{1}=2^{n/2-1}(2^{n/2}+1)
and
v_{1}=2^{n/2-1}(2^{n/2}-1)
, and hence
2^{n/2}-1\mid(k_{1},v_{1})
since
b_{1}k_{1}=v_{1}r_{1}
. On the
other hand,
(k_{1},v_{1})\mid A+1
by Lemma
3.7
(1), hence
A\geqslant 2^{n/2}-2
. Moreover,
\frac{v_{0}-1}{k_{0}-1}>\left(2^{n/2-1}(2^{n/2}-1)\right)^{1/2}
by Theorem
7.1
(3). So

(2^{n/2}+1)\left(2^{n/2-1}-1\right)=A\frac{v_{0}-1}{k_{0}-1}>\left(2^{n/2}-2\right)\left(2^{n/2-1}(2^{n/2}-1)\right)^{1/2}\text{,}

which is impossible for
n\geqslant 14
.

Claim 4
.

G_{(\Sigma)}=1
.

Assume that
M\in\mathcal{C}_{1}(T)
. If
M
is the stabilizer of a nondegenerate subspace of
PG_{n-1}(2)
, then
2^{n^{2}/4-i^{2}/4-(n-i)^{2}/4}

divides
\left|T:M\right|
for some
1\leqslant i<n/2
, and hence
v_{1}(v_{1}-1)
, by
[
34
, Proposition 4.1.3(II)]
. Then
n^{2}/4-i^{2}/4-(n-i)^{2}/4\leqslant n/2-1
since
v_{1}=2^{n/2-1}(2^{n/2}+\varepsilon 1)
, hence
3/2+(i-1)n/2\leqslant 0
, contrary to
i\geqslant 1
. Thus
M

is a maximal parabolic subgroup of
T
of type
P_{h}
for some
1\leqslant h\leqslant n/2
, and hence

(8.17)

\prod_{j=0}^{h-1}\frac{2^{n-2j}-1}{2^{j+1}-1}\mid 2^{n/2-1}(2^{n}-1)\left(2^{n/2-1}-\varepsilon 1\right)

by
[
34
, Proposition 4.1.19(II)]
since
\left|T:M\right|\mid v_{1}(v_{1}-1)
and
v_{1}(v_{1}-1)=2^{n/2-1}(2^{n}-1)(2^{n/2-1}-\varepsilon 1)
. Now
1\leqslant h\leqslant n/2
and
n\geqslant 14

imply
h<n-4
, and hence the first part of (
8.17
) is
divisible by primitive prime divisor
2^{n-4}-1
for
h\geqslant 3
, whereas the second part of (
8.17
) is not. Thus
h=1
or
2
. Also, if
h=2
, then (
8.17
) implies
\frac{1}{3}\left(2^{n-2}-1\right)\left(2^{n}-1\right)\mid(2^{n}-1)\left(2^{n/2-1}+\varepsilon 1\right)
, which is impossible since
n\geqslant 14
. Therefore
h=1
, and hence
M\cong Y:Sp_{n-2}(2)
with
Y\cong[2^{n-1}]
by
[
34
, Proposition 4.1.19(II)]
. Therefore
\left|M:T_{B^{\Sigma}}\right|=\frac{1}{\delta}2^{n/2-1}\left(2^{n/2-1}-\varepsilon 1\right)
for some
\delta\geqslant 1
, and hence

\displaystyle(2^{n-2}-1)\cdot\left|T_{B^{\Sigma}}Y:T_{B^{\Sigma}}\right|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle P(M/Y)\cdot\left|T_{B^{\Sigma}}Y:T_{B^{\Sigma}}\right|

\displaystyle\leqslant

\displaystyle\left|M/Y:T_{B^{\Sigma}}Y/Y\right|\cdot\left|T_{B^{\Sigma}}Y:T_{B^{\Sigma}}\right|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left|M:T_{B^{\Sigma}}Y\right|\cdot\left|T_{B^{\Sigma}}Y:T_{B^{\Sigma}}\right|=\left|M:T_{B^{\Sigma}}\right|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\delta}2^{n/2-1}\left(2^{n/2-1}-\varepsilon 1\right)

Thus
\varepsilon=-1
,
Y\leqslant T_{B^{\Sigma}}
and
\left|M/Y:T_{B^{\Sigma}}/Y\right|=2^{n/2}(2^{n/2}+1)
. Consequently,

(8.18)

\frac{b_{1}}{\theta}=\left|T:T_{B^{\Sigma}}\right|=\left|T:M\right|\cdot\left|M/Y:T_{B^{\Sigma}}/Y\right|=2^{n/2-1}\left(2^{n}-1\right)\left(2^{n/2-1}+1\right)

for some
\theta\geqslant 1
since
T\unlhd G^{\Sigma}
and
G^{\Sigma}
acts block-transitively on
\mathcal{D}_{1}
. That is,
b_{1}=\theta\cdot v_{1}(v_{1}-1)
, and hence
A\cdot\theta\cdot\frac{k_{1}}{\lambda}=1
since
A\cdot b_{1}\cdot\frac{k_{1}}{\lambda}=v_{1}(v_{1}-1)
by Lemma
6.1
(2). Thus
A=\theta=1
and
k_{1}=\lambda
. On the other hand, Now,
\lambda\mid\left|T_{\Delta}\right|
since
\lambda\nmid\left|Out(T)\right|
by Lemma
8.2
(2) and
\lambda\nmid v_{1}
. Thus
\lambda\mid 2^{n-2i}-1
with
i>1
since with
T_{\Delta}\cong O_{n}^{-}(2)
and
\lambda\nmid v_{1}-1
. In particular,
\lambda\leqslant 2^{n-4}-1
since
n\geqslant 14
. Then

2^{n/2-1}(2^{n/2}-1)=v_{1}<2k_{1}=2\lambda<2^{n-3}\text{,}

which is impossible for
n\geqslant 14
.

Claim 5
.

\mathcal{D}_{0}
is a
2
-
(v_{0},k_{0},\lambda)
design with
A\frac{v_{0}-1}{k_{0}-1}=(2^{n/2}+1)\left(2^{n/2-1}-1\right)
,
G_{(\Delta)}=1
and
\Omega_{n}^{\varepsilon}(2)\unlhd G_{\Delta}\leqslant GO_{n}^{\varepsilon}(2)
.

It follows from Claim 4 that
T_{\Delta}\cong O_{n}^{\varepsilon}(2)
and

. If

, then

, and hence

by

, whereas

by Lemma

. Thus, one has

and
\Omega_{n}^{\varepsilon}(2)\unlhd G_{\Delta}\leqslant GO_{n}^{\varepsilon}(2)
.

If
\mu=\lambda
, then either

or

is a linear
space by Lemma

(2). In the first case,

acts
point-
2

, and hence either

,
\varepsilon=-
and hence

, or

,

and

(e.g. see

, Proposition 2.9.1]
) since

,
whereas

. Therefore

,

,

and

, or
\mathcal{D}_{0}\cong PG_{5}(2)
,

,

by

, which is not the case since

. Thus

, and hence
\mathcal{D}_{0}
is a
2
-
(v_{0},k_{0},\lambda)
design by Lemma
2.7
(2). Moreover

by Proposition

(3).

Claim 6
.

Set
K=\left(T_{\Delta}\right)^{\prime}
, then
\left|K_{x}\right|^{2}>\left|K\right|
. In particular,
K_{x}
is a
large subgroup of
K
.

Set
K=\left(T_{\Delta}\right)^{\prime}
. Then
K\cong\Omega_{n}^{\varepsilon}(2)
since
K
acts faithfully on
\mathcal{D}_{0}
by Claim 5.

\left|K:K_{x}\right|=v_{0}
since
K\trianglelefteq G_{\Delta}
and
G_{\Delta}

acts point-primitively on
\mathcal{D}_{0}
. If
C
is any block of
\mathcal{D}_{0}
containing

, then
\left|K_{x}:K_{x,C}\right|\mid\frac{v_{0}-1}{k_{0}-1}\lambda
with
r_{0}=\frac{v_{0}-1}{k_{0}-1}\lambda
since
K_{x}\unlhd G_{x}
. Therefore,

\left|K\right|=v_{0}\left|K_{x}\right|=v_{0}\frac{\left(v_{0}-1\right)\lambda}{e\left(k_{0}-1\right)}\left|K_{x,B}\right|

for some integer
e\geqslant 1
. If
\left|K_{x,B}\right|\leqslant e
, by Claim 5, one has

\displaystyle\left|K\right|

\displaystyle\leqslant

\displaystyle\left(\frac{(2^{n/2}+1)\left(2^{n/2-1}-1\right)(k_{0}-1)}{A}+1\right)\frac{(2^{n/2}+1)\left(2^{n/2-1}-1\right)}{A}\lambda

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{(2^{n/2}+1)^{2}\left(2^{n/2-1}-1\right)^{2}(k_{0}-1)}{A^{2}}\lambda+\frac{(2^{n/2}+1)\left(2^{n/2-1}-1\right)}{A}\lambda

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(2^{n/2}+1)^{2}\left(2^{n/2-1}-1\right)^{2}\lambda^{2}+(2^{n/2}+1)\left(2^{n/2-1}-1\right)\lambda

\displaystyle\leqslant

\displaystyle(2^{n/2}+1)^{2}\left(2^{n/2-1}-1\right)^{2}(2^{n/2}+1)^{2}+(2^{n/2}+1)\left(2^{n/2-1}-1\right)(2^{n/2}+1)\text{.}

Then

\frac{1}{8}2^{n(n-1)/2}<(2^{n/2}+1)^{2}\left(2^{n/2-1}-1\right)^{2}(2^{n/2}+1)^{2}+(2^{n/2}+1)\left(2^{n/2-1}-1\right)(2^{n/2}+1)

by
[
1
, Corollary 4.3(iv)]
since
n\geqslant 14
, and we reach a contradiction. Therefore
\left|K_{x,B}\right|>e
, and so

\left|K_{x}\right|=\frac{\left(v_{0}-1\right)\lambda}{e\left(k_{0}-1\right)}\left|K_{x,B}\right|>\frac{v_{0}-1}{k_{0}-1}\lambda=\frac{v_{0}-1}{k_{0}-1}\lambda\geqslant v_{0}-1\text{,}

and hence
\left|K_{x}\right|>v_{0}
since
\lambda\mid\left|K_{x}\right|
and
\lambda\nmid v_{0}
. Then
\left|K_{x}\right|^{2}>v_{0}\left|K_{x}\right|=\left|K\right|
, and hence
K_{x}
is a large subgroup of
K
.

Claim 7
.

The final contradiction.

Let
N
be a maximal subgroup of
K
containing
K_{x}
. Then
N
is one of
the groups listed in
[
1
, Proposition 4.23 and Table 7]
since

is a large subgroup of

by Claim 6. Actually,
N\in\mathcal{C}_{1}(K)\cup\mathcal{C}_{2}(K)\cup\mathcal{C}_{3}(K)
since

\left|N\right|^{2}>\left|K\right|
and
n\geqslant 14
. If
N\notin\mathcal{C}_{1}(K)
, then one of the following holds by
[
